### THE
# PERSECUTIONS
# OF THE RIGHTEOUS:
#### Or
### Letters written by the late Miss
## ANTOINETTE BOURIGNON,
<note>Antoinette Bourignon (1616–1680) was a controversial Flemish mystic who claimed to receive direct revelations from God. She fled from city to city across Europe to escape religious authorities.</note>

*Occasioned by all sorts of persecutions which*
*greeted her in every place and at every time, until*
*her death, because of the truths of God that she an-*
*nounced to men by His command.*

<meta>This page continues from the previous epigraph (1 John 3:13), providing a formal introduction to the reader. <meta>catchword: One</meta>

# NOTICE TO THE READER.

Those who are acquainted with the principles and the truths of the Gospel are well aware that the natural state of the Righteous in this world is Persecution. If they chose to remain silent, or to speak only of vain and foolish things—even wicked and impious ones; if they wished to lead a pagan, worldly, and entirely vice-filled life, they would be left in peace without anyone saying or doing anything to them, except perhaps to flatter and praise them in their vanity. But as soon as one seeks to withdraw from evil, to speak the truth and practice it, they are exposed—as a prophet says—to plunder; they are hated, and even, if possible, put to death by the Priests <term>Sacrificateurs: literally "sacrificers," referring here to religious authorities who preserve the status quo through ritual or power rather than spirit</term> themselves, between the Temple and the Altar. <note>This is a biblical allusion to Matthew 23:35, referring to the martyrdom of Zechariah, used here to suggest that the most dangerous persecution often comes from within the church hierarchy.</note>

Examples of this are found everywhere and in every century. Those who have read the *Life of **Miss BOURIGNON*** <note>Antoinette Bourignon (1616–1680) was a Flemish mystic who claimed direct revelation from God. Her radical views led to her being hunted by both Catholic and Protestant authorities.</note> will see a notable example from our own time, which has perhaps had no equal—whether one considers the rage of her enemies, its duration, its malice, or the object of their animosity, who was merely a weak, solitary girl; or whether one looks at the cause, the persecutors themselves, or finally a thousand other rare and remarkable circumstances. The reading of the following letters may serve to illustrate these points, as well as those in the Second Part of the *Testimony of Truth* <note>original: "Temoignage de Verité"</note>, where her persecutions in **HOLSTEIN** <note>A region in Northern Germany where Bourignon attempted to establish a spiritual community and printing press, only to be driven out by Lutheran clergy.</note> are recounted, and where a Preface has been included that will exempt me from elaborating further here.

<summary>This preface introduces Antoinette Bourignon's letters by framing her legal and social persecution as a spiritual inevitability for anyone following the "true Gospel." It specifically points the reader toward her accounts of being hounded by religious authorities in Holstein.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Persecution, Gospel truth, Holstein, Testimony of Truth, religious conflict, martyr narrative</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial introduction, transitioning from the general necessity of Christian suffering to the specific arrangement of Antoinette Bourignon's correspondence. It identifies her letters as a chronological record of her life and her shared trials with her associate, Christian de Cort.</meta>

<meta>catchword: un</meta>

# NOTICE.

These letters have been arranged according to the chronological order of their dates because of their bearing on the history of her Life. Since her life was entirely one of persecutions, the principal periods of it could not fail to be marked here. The first letters, addressed to *Mr. de Cort* <note>Christian de Cort (d. 1669) was a Catholic priest and Superior of the Oratory in Mechelen. He became a follower of Bourignon and attempted to establish a spiritual community with her on the island of Nordstrand.</note>, highlight the afflictions of this *Lady* <note>original: "Damlle." (Mademoiselle), referring to Antoinette Bourignon.</note> no less than those of that good man. He was targeted primarily for the love of the Truth that he had embraced; all other reasons were merely secondary motives or pretexts that moved his enemies to act as a consequence of this primary one. If one wishes to know the details of this more particularly, one need only read what is reported in the *History of the Life of Miss Bourignon*, as well as in *Innocence Recognized*, joined to the Second Part of the *Testimony of Truth*, and in the third part of *Light Born in Darkness*, from letter 31 to 35.

<margin>(a) 2 Tim. 3. (b) Hebr. 10.</margin>
The small dots ..... which occasionally appear in her letters, whether here or in some other volume, indicate that something has been omitted from her letter in that place—a proper name, or some specific detail of household <term>menage: domestic management or family affairs</term> or other business that was of no importance for anyone to know for their edification. The rest vividly reflects those words of *St. Paul*: (a) *Those who desire to live a pious life according to Jesus Christ shall suffer persecutions.* <note>original: "Ceux qui veulent vivre dans la piété ſelon Jeſus Chriſt, ſouffriront des perſecutions." A translation of 2 Timothy 3:12.</note> But is it not better, along with *Moses*, (b) *to choose to be afflicted for* <note>This sentence breaks off at the end of the page. It is a reference to Hebrews 11:25: "Choosing rather to suffer affliction with the people of God." Note that the original margin cites Heb. 10, though the Moses reference is traditionally found in chapter 11.</note>

<summary>The editor explains that the letters are organized chronologically to document Antoinette Bourignon's life of persecution and notes that personal or mundane details have been redacted to focus on spiritual edification.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Christian de Cort, religious persecution, correspondence, hagiography, 2 Timothy 3:12, Moses, spiritual biography</keywords>

# NOTICE.

<margin>(c) Matthew 5. (d) John ch. 15. & ch. 16.</margin>

*...a time with the people of* God, *than to enjoy the short-*
*lived pleasures of sin and the prosperities of* Egypt <note>A reference to Hebrews 11:24-26, where Moses chooses to suffer with the Israelites rather than live as royalty; Bourignon frequently used this as a model for her own life.</note>,
*which will have such a fatal and sudden end?* God
*grant us the grace to also make this good choice, and*
*to prefer the reproaches of* Christ *to the treasures of the*
*world, looking toward the eternal reward that*
Jesus Christ *promised, saying to his children, (c)* Blessed
*are those who are persecuted for righteousness' sake: for*
*the* Kingdom of Heaven *is theirs. You shall be blessed*
*when they have reviled and persecuted you, and*
*when for my sake they have said against you, falsely,*
*all kinds of evil. Rejoice then and*
*leap for joy: for your reward is great in*

*who were before you. (d) If the world hates you, know*
*that it hated me before it hated you. If you were of the world, the*
*world would love its own: but because you*
*are not of the world and because I have chosen you from it, it is*
*for this that the world hates you. Remember*
*the word that I said to you, The servant is not*
*greater than his* Master. *If they have persecuted me, they*
*will also persecute you. And if they have kept my word,*
*they will also keep yours. But they will do all*
*these things to you because of my* Name, *because they do not know*
*the one who sent me. — indeed the time is coming*
*that whoever kills you will think they are rendering ser-*
*vice to* God. *They will do these things because they*
*have known neither the* Father *nor me. But I have told you these*
*things so that when the hour comes you may re-*
*member that I told them to you.*

<meta>This page serves as a formal preface or "Notice" to the collection of letters. It follows the introductory discussion on the inevitability of persecution for the righteous, transitioning into the specific editorial choices made for this volume and the historical context of Antoinette Bourignon’s correspondence with Christian de Cort.</meta>
<meta>catchword: a</meta>

-># NOTICE.<-

These letters have been arranged according to the chronological order of their dates because of their connection to the history of her Life; since her life was entirely one of persecutions, it is inevitable that the principal periods of it are marked here. The first letters, which are addressed to Mr. de *Cort*, <note>Christian de Cort (1608–1669) was a Catholic priest and superior of the Oratory in Mechelen. He became a devoted follower of Bourignon and died in her service while fleeing legal troubles in Holstein.</note> show the afflictions of this Young Lady <note>referring to Antoinette Bourignon</note> no less than those of this good man, who was targeted primarily for the sake of the Truth he had embraced—all other reasons being merely secondary motives or pretexts that prompted his enemies to act as a consequence of this primary cause. If one wishes to know the details of this more specifically, one need only read what is reported in the history of the *Life of Miss Bourignon*, as well as in *Innocence Recognized*, joined to the Second Part of the *Testimony of Truth*, and in the third part of *Light Born in Darkness*, from letter 31 to 35. <note>These titles refer to the extensive series of apologetic and autobiographical works published by Bourignon and her followers to defend her mystical teachings against ecclesiastical condemnation.</note>

<margin>(a) 2 Timothy 3. (b) Hebrews 10.</margin>
The little dots ..... that sometimes appear in her letters, whether here or in any other volume, indicate that something has been omitted from her letter in that place—a proper name, or some specific detail of household or other business <term>menage: referring to domestic or administrative affairs of her community</term> that was of no importance for anyone to know for their edification: and the rest vividly marks these words of St. Paul: (a) *Those who wish to live in godliness according to Jesus Christ will suffer persecutions.* <note>original: "Ceux qui veulent vivre dans la piété selon Jesus Christ, souffriront des persécutions" (2 Timothy 3:12).</note> But is it not better, with Moses, (b) *to choose to be afflicted for* <note>The text breaks off here, referencing Hebrews 11:25 (though the margin says Heb 10), where Moses chooses to suffer with the people of God rather than enjoy the fleeting pleasures of sin.</note>

<summary>This preface explains the chronological organization of Antoinette Bourignon's letters and their focus on her life of persecution. It also provides an editorial guide regarding omissions made for the reader's "edification."</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Christian de Cort, religious persecution, spiritual edification, 17th-century mysticism, editorial practices</keywords>

# NOTICE.

*...for a time, with the people of* God, *than to enjoy the short-lived pleasures of sin and the prosperities of* Egypt, *which will have such a fatal and sudden end?* <note>The author is paraphrasing Hebrews 11:25-26, comparing the choice of the religious seeker to that of Moses, who chose suffering with the Israelites over the luxury of Pharaoh's court.</note> God *give us the grace to also make this good choice, and to prefer the reproaches of* Christ *to the treasures of the world, looking toward the eternal reward that* Jesus Christ *has promised, saying to his children,* (c) *Blessed are those who are persecuted for righteousness: for the* Kingdom of Heaven *is theirs. You will be blessed when men revile you and persecute you, and when for my sake they say all kinds of evil against you falsely. Rejoice then and leap for joy: for your reward is great in* Heaven: *for this is how they persecuted the* Prophets *who were before you.* (d) *If the world hates you, know that it hated me before you. If you were of the world, the world would love its own: but because you are not of the world and I have chosen you out of it, that is why the world hates you. Remember the word that I said to you: The servant is not greater than his* Master. *If they have persecuted me, they will persecute you also. And if they have kept my word, they will keep yours as well. But they will do all these things to you because of my* Name, *because they do not know the one who sent me. — the time is even coming when whoever puts you to death will think they are rendering a service to* God. *They will do these things to you because they have known neither the* Father *nor me. But I have told you these things so that when the hour has come, you may remember that I told them to you.*

(c) Matthew 5. (d) John ch. 15 & ch. 16.

# T A B L E
## Of the Letters in this Volume.

---

### FIRST LETTER.
->To Mr. DE CORT,<- <note>Christian de Cort (d. 1669) was a Dutch priest and close collaborator of Antoinette Bourignon; he was instrumental in publishing her works and helping her establish her spiritual community.</note>

<margin>I.</margin> The wicked are more zealous in upholding evil and fighting against the good than the good are for the opposite. God’s designs are unchangeable. The soul is deprived of the presence of God after death for not having advanced the work of God. Oppositions from the Devil: the help of God is more powerful. page 1

<margin>II.</margin> The best people, instead of supporting the truth, abandon it, succumbing to the temptations of the Devil. Truth and virtue are currently hated, persecuted, and a cause of scandal even to the righteous. One must simply resolve to suffer in patience. page 2

<margin>III.</margin> Concerning a persecutor of her person and that of Mr. de Cort, who tried to draw innocent words from one and good actions from the other as a subject to ruin and calumniate them, in order to hinder the designs of God and the progress of the truth. page 5

<margin>IV.</margin> Description of one of her female persecutors and a notable conversation with her, from which the other tried to find grounds for surprise and accusation. page 10

<margin>V.</margin> The Devil deceives men with false peace: he has great power over them. The <term>Reformation</term> <note>In Bourignon's context, "Reformation" refers to a mystical, internal restoration of true Christianity, rather than solely the historical Protestant Reformation.</note> will not be accomplished by the great and the learned, but by the small and the simple: the former being more often mockers and persecutors of divine truth, which they wish to subject to their human sciences. We must seek only the glory of God, even if it meant being confounded before men-

<summary>This page provides a table of contents for the first letter in the volume, addressed to Christian de Cort. It outlines themes of spiritual struggle, the corruption of the learned, and the inevitable persecution faced by those seeking divine truth.</summary>
<keywords>Christian de Cort, spiritual warfare, divine providence, persecution, religious reformation, humility versus scholarship, Antoinette Bourignon</keywords>

men. page 17

<margin>VI.</margin> To the same, actually persecuted and imprisoned for the truth, though under vain and false temporal pretexts, in which his persecutors also found their own worldly advantages. Advice on conduct toward enemies. page 21

<margin>VII.</margin> To the same concerning the first two letters of *Recognized Innocence*. <note>original: "l'innocence reconnue." This refers to Bourignon's published defense of her character and mission.</note> The incompatibility of good and evil. To do nothing for the sake of the person, but everything for the truth. Consulting God on all things. page 25

<margin>VIII.</margin> To the same, [stating] that those who appear most devout are without charity, and others are full of malice and treachery: it is better to wait upon God without human means than to use the wicked only to ruin oneself further. page 29

<margin>IX.</margin> To the same; divine and admirable advice concerning the manner of using men and human means. page 31

<margin>X.</margin> To the same. One must not follow human <term>prudence</term> <note>In this mystical context, "human prudence" refers to worldly wisdom, caution, or self-interest as opposed to divine inspiration.</note>, but the Spirit of God. It is better to make mistakes before men while following the Spirit of justice and truth, than to make them before God by following human considerations: because consolation and correction follow the former, while the latter rack the conscience. The utility of suffering. page 32

<margin>XI.</margin> To the same. The hatred the Devil has for the light triggers persecutions. One must abandon oneself absolutely to God while suffering for justice. page 36

<margin>XII.</margin> To the same. The perfidy of men. Universal perils. God’s assistance: in what kind of perils it makes itself known. page 38

<margin>XIII.</margin> To the same. Treachery of men: the weakness of some, the lack of charity in others. One must conquer through suffering. page 43

<meta>This page begins the main text of "The Persecutions of the Just," following the table of contents. # THE
# PERSECUTIONS
# OF THE JUST.
<note>The "Just" (French: *le Juste*) refers to the righteous person or the innocent sufferer who follows God's will despite worldly opposition.</note>

## LETTER I.

### To Mr. de Cort.
<note>Christian de Cort was a Superior of the Oratory in Mechelen and a close collaborator of Bourignon; he shared her vision of a reformed Christian life and helped fund her printing endeavors.</note>

*The wicked are more fervent in supporting evil and fighting against good than the righteous are for the opposite. God’s designs are immutable. A soul deprived of God’s presence after death for failing to advance God’s work. Opposition from the Devil: God’s help is more powerful.*

---

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'N' with elaborate foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>

Not having received news from you, I assumed you are resolved to come this way: which seems to me to be the will of God: because so many men employ themselves in supporting the Devil's side, it is necessary that those who wish to belong to God also support His side <note>original: "partie," meaning cause or faction.</note>; for as few as there are of them, they are not as zealous or fervent as their adversaries, who work night and day through remarkable inventions <note>original: "inventions admirables," likely referring to clever schemes or deceptive plots.</note> to serve such a wicked master. I fear that they will reproach the others for their laziness on the day of judgment.

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: 2. But</meta>

<vocab>Persecutions, Just, de Cort, God, Devil, Soul, Judgment, Zealous</vocab>

<summary>This opening letter to Christian de Cort emphasizes the urgency of supporting God's cause in a world where the wicked work tirelessly for the Devil. Bourignon warns that the righteous lack the fervor of their enemies and may face judgment for their spiritual laziness.</summary>
<keywords>Christian de Cort, Antoinette Bourignon, spiritual warfare, divine will, Day of Judgment, zeal, the Devil, righteousness</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the first letter to Christian de Cort, dated December 9, 1654, and begins the second letter. The text focuses on the spiritual struggle against the "Devil's side" and the failures of the clergy and "good people" to uphold the truth.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>The Persecutions</header>

2. But God will not fail to accomplish His designs for men. If any make themselves unworthy of them, He will take the talent away from them to give it to others. <note>A reference to the Biblical Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25), suggesting that spiritual gifts or responsibilities are reassigned if not used for God’s work.</note>

3. I felt your late little Archbishop <note>original: "petit Archévéque." Bourignon often used "little" as a diminutive for clergy she felt lacked true spiritual stature. This likely refers to a recently deceased local prelate.</note> sighing still. I fear that his deprivation <note>In this context, "deprivation" refers to the soul being denied the "Beatific Vision" or presence of God after death due to unfinished spiritual work or failures during life.</note> will last until God’s hour has been born, for he seems to long for it; if he did not do it in his lifetime, others must make up for it.

4. It seems that the Devil blocks every entrance, so as not to allow any place for the truth to be known.

5. For the rest, God is above all; let us only do what we can on our part.

This 9th of Dec. 1654.

---

## LETTER II.

*The best of people, instead of supporting the truth, abandon it, succumbing to the temptations of the Devil. Truth and virtue are currently hated, persecuted, and even a scandal to good people. One must simply resolve to suffer in patience.*

->SIR,<-

1. I have received yours without a date, and heard that Mr. C. says he cannot give me instruction to prepare the Justifica-

# of the Just. Letter 2.

<meta>catchword: will</meta>

tion: <note>This syllable completes the word "Justification" from the previous page, referring to a formal defense of her work.</note> which makes me fear (along with many other reasons) that he no longer wishes to involve himself in God’s work. <note>original: "l'affaire de Dieu." Bourignon frequently refers to her mission and the establishment of her spiritual community as "the affair" or "the business" of God.</note> This causes me much grief; not out of fear for the success of the work (because the same God who wills it can, in an instant, overthrow all adversaries, and even the entire world, to fulfill His designs); but because he deprives himself of the great lights and favors of God which he would have received in this service; seeing that He had chosen him for this, to the exclusion of so many others. The Oratorian Fathers <note>The Oratory of Jesus, a Catholic congregation of priests. Bourignon often clashed with organized religious orders, believing they prioritized human rules over divine inspiration.</note> have held the same power over his soul as the Jesuit Fathers once held over that of Van der Burch <note>François van der Burch (1567–1644), the Archbishop of Cambrai. Bourignon implies that he was a man of God who became "erased" from spiritual favor because he followed the Jesuits' counsel rather than God's direct light.</note>, with only this difference: that the aforementioned Archbishop was erased from my memory as soon as he stopped listening to God and lent his ear to men who contradicted Him; whereas Mr. C. is not forgotten by me, appearing always in my spirit, though with pain and a pressing weight upon my heart.

2. I pray to God that He preserves him: for the Devil is wicked: if he cannot have full power over his soul, he would at least have it over his body and his mind, as he had over a servant of God, whom I had often warned to guard himself against certain people: which he did not do, believing it was more just to consider as good what I suspected was evil: this wisdom of his <note>Bourignon often uses "wisdom" ironically to describe human intellectualism or "common sense" that ignores spiritual intuition.</note> gave the Devil power to ruin both his health and his judgment in large part. God wishes to have nothing by force: if it is not of [the]

She warns that choosing human "wisdom" over divine warning allows the Devil to ruin one's health and mind.</summary>

will to pursue what he has begun, do not press him; and if I am a cause of offense to him, speak of me to him no more until he receives further light. <note>In Bourignon’s theology, "light" (lumières) refers to direct spiritual insight or divine revelation granted by God.</note>

3. Miss M. <note>Likely one of Bourignon's female followers or associates.</note> left on Tuesday for Dendermonde, <note>French: Termonde. A city in Flanders, modern-day Belgium.</note> and from there to Mechelen. <note>French: Malines. This refers to her legal and spiritual legacy and the instructions for her community.</note> were it not for the indisposition of Mr. C., who offered himself no more for that purpose than the other did. I will wait a little longer, even though my health is not as it usually is, without being altogether sick. The aforementioned woman felt some dissatisfaction with me before her departure: bad dogs usually bite the stone thrown at them, and haughty people would gladly bite those who correct them. I am in her house in solitude, according to my desire...

4. If I am hated in Mechelen, it will be better for me to absent myself from there, as I did from Lille; since Jesus Christ says: *if they persecute you in one city, flee to another.* <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 10:23.</note> The Devil has a terrible power over human spirits, even over the most virtuous of the present time. This is what was predicted by Jesus Christ, *that many will be offended*; <note>original: "scandalizés." In a 17th-century context, this implies being led into sin or turning away from faith due to a "stumbling block." Reference to Matthew 24:10.</note> even the most upright people today act against the truth and true virtue. It is thus throughout the whole world. One must possess one’s soul in patience, awaiting the glorious coming of Jesus Christ.

This July 11, 1665.

of the Just. Letter 3.

# LETTER III.

*Concerning a Persecutor of her person and that of Mr. de Cort, who tried to extract innocent words from the one and good actions from the other, as a pretext to ruin and calumniate them, in order to hinder God's designs and the progress of the truth.*

---

<margin>1.</margin> I spoke only once in my life to Father V.L. It was around June 1666, in the house of Mrs. V., in the presence of Father Jean, the aforementioned Mrs. M. St. G., and the <term>Regent of the foundlings</term><note>The "enfants trouvés" or foundlings refers to the orphanage in Lille that Antoinette Bourignon managed. The "Regent" was the head administrator.</note>. He made his doctrine known by citing much and listening little to others.

<margin>2.</margin> They spoke of virtue, saying that one did not know where to seek it. He said *that there were still many virtuous people among the <term>Religious Orders</term><note>original: "Religions." In this period, the term referred specifically to organized monastic orders or congregations like the Jesuits or Franciscans.</note>.* I said that the Spirit of God had departed from them ever since greed had entered in; that they were very far from the Spirit of their Founders, who had placed them in the fields, away from the noise of the world, to enjoy solitude and apply themselves to contemplation; whereas now they occupied themselves with the affairs of the earth.

<margin>3.</margin> He said that God's Providence had so ordained it, that they should come into the cities in order to instruct and give a good example to the people. I said that it had rather been the Devil who had made them desire the cities, to encourage the slackening of their first <term>fervor</term><note>A common spiritual term for the initial intense heat of religious devotion.</note> and to lose their devotion.

<summary>Bourignon recounts a 1666 debate with a priest regarding the corruption of monastic orders, arguing that they abandoned their founders' vision of rural solitude for the worldly greed of the cities. This letter serves as a defense against those who sought to use her words to brand her a heretic.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Christian de Cort, monasticism, religious corruption, Lille, foundlings, Father V.L.</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Antoinette Bourignon’s conversation with Father V.L. and Sister G., specifically debating the relative virtue of cloistered life versus secular marriage, and addressing contemporary fears of witchcraft.</meta>

### The Persecutions

tion, without benefiting others: and that there was more piety and virtue to be found among secular persons, who lived by their labor in simplicity and justice, than among the cloisters of today, which are without Charity.

<margin>4.</margin> To which the said Virgin <note>original: "laditte V." Antoinette Bourignon frequently referred to herself as "the Virgin" to emphasize her status as an unmarried woman dedicated to God.</note> said to the said Sister G.: "Get married, since virtue is found more in marriage than in the cloisters." To which she replied: "I have indeed seen the experience of that in my own mother, who was much more virtuous than I." Then I spoke again, saying that I did not mean to say that marriage was more perfect than <term>Religious Life</term><note>original: "la Religion." In this context, it refers to the formal state of being a monk or nun within a recognized order.</note> in itself—for it brings many distractions and <insert>hindrances</insert> to virtue—but that I meant in the practice of today, where it seems that Nuns and <term>devout women</term><note>original: "dévotes," often referring to laywomen who took private vows or lived a life of intense religious practice outside a formal convent.</note> believe they have enough virtue as long as they do not sleep with a man, even though they are full of all sorts of vicious passions.

<margin>5.</margin> Father J. admitted this regarding the simple people of the world, for he saw it in their confessions. They then began to speak of how the Devil had great power over men today; that it was being said everywhere that there were now so many sorcerers, even within the cloisters. To this Father V. L. said: that these were merely tales and imaginings; that he made no account of all that was said about witches; nor did Mr. Councilor of the Privy Council, who would not listen to such talk; and that everything said to be <term>sorcery</term><note>original: "maléfice," specifically referring to harm or charms caused by witchcraft.</note> was actually <term>sympathy</term><note>In the 17th century, "sympathy" was a technical term used to describe a natural, hidden connection between physical objects, often used by skeptics to explain away supposedly "magical" events through natural philosophy.</note> occurring through natural means; this...

<summary>Antoinette Bourignon argues that secular life and marriage can be more virtuous than corrupt monastic orders, while Father V.L. expresses skepticism regarding the contemporary panic over witchcraft, attributing it to natural phenomena.</summary>
<keywords>monasticism, marriage vs. celibacy, virtue, hypocrisy, witchcraft, sorcery, natural philosophy, Father V.L., religious corruption</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Antoinette Bourignon’s account of her dispute with religious authorities, specifically Father V. L., following the conversation about the corruption of cloisters and the power of the devil mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

<header>On the Just. Letter 3.</header>

which Father J. admitted: and for my part, I remained silent.

6. This, in short, is everything that was said in this conference, which was the first and the last that I had with the aforementioned V. L. Nevertheless, he accuses me of <term>heresy</term><note>The formal denial or doubt of a core doctrine of the Christian faith, a very serious charge in the 17th century that could lead to legal prosecution.</note>, and says that if he were <term>Vicar General</term><note>A high-ranking administrative deputy to a bishop, possessing significant legal and disciplinary power.</note>, he would certainly forbid me from speaking to the Father or to others. He told the Virgin <note>original: "V." referring to Antoinette Bourignon herself, who often used this title.</note> that my sentiments are condemned by the <term>Councils</term><note>The formal assemblies of Church leaders, such as the Council of Trent, which defined Catholic orthodoxy.</note>, and that I have made a fool of the Father: and he is writing about it to Paris: he speaks of it to the Councilors of Brussels, to the Pastor of Levedal, to the Doctors of <term>Louvain</term><note>The Catholic University of Louvain was the primary center for theological study and the enforcement of religious orthodoxy in the Spanish Netherlands.</note>, in order to make me hated, and to discredit the Father on all sides, portraying him as a <term>fantastic</term><note>In this period, "fantastic" meant someone driven by delusions or "fancies" rather than reason or divine truth.</note> and a visionary, and me as a fool and a heretic.

7. Should this be tolerated by his superiors, who well know the contrary? Should they not know his reasons, so as to warn us; or else make him keep silent, without stripping honor from those he ought to respect, thus authorizing everyone to despise their <term>Pastor</term><note>A spiritual leader or parish priest; here likely referring to the religious figure supporting Bourignon.</note>, who is a public person, and me likewise, for having publicly taught? He who believes he has the love of God, and does not defend justice and innocence, is deceived: for Truth and Justice are God; and He dwells in all souls where this Truth and Justice reside. He who does not esteem and support them cannot have the love of God; because he cannot prove this love except by the love he bears for Justice and Truth: since God in His essence cannot be seen or known; but rather...

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: se</meta>

<summary>Antoinette Bourignon recounts the aftermath of her meeting with Father V. L., detailing his attempts to destroy her reputation by accusing her of heresy and reporting her to various religious authorities across Europe. She argues that true love for God is inseparable from the defense of justice and truth.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, heresy, Vicar General, Councils, Louvain, religious persecution, justice, truth, 17th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Antoinette Bourignon’s defense against the accusations of Father V. L. It follows her explanation that God’s presence should be recognized through the spiritual fruits of a person's life rather than through formal ecclesiastical approval alone.</meta>

### The Persecutions.

...to make Himself felt through the gifts He infuses into our souls—of faith and charity—which are likewise spiritual and invisible, yet nevertheless make themselves known through our visible actions, by loving and upholding justice and truth wherever they may be found, without any regard or respect for persons <note>original: "sans aucun égard ou respect des personnes," a biblical sentiment meaning showing no favoritism based on social status or rank.</note>.

8. This being established as it is, how can one suffer in good conscience that the aforementioned *Father V. L.* attacks me with calumnies or attacks the truth, which is God, without any foundation or reasons whatsoever? Since, having required him to declare them, he confesses to knowing none, except for some hearsay from here and there. Is this not a fine pretext to have a person examined in a <term>Vicariate</term><note>The administrative and legal office of a Vicar General, used here to refer to the ecclesiastical court or the official scrutiny of the church hierarchy.</note>, and to make her suspected of crimes? No one can love God without resenting this insult: because it concerns His honor, as I have been sent by Him to declare His will to His servants.

9. This enemy, in order to discredit my <term>commission</term><note>Bourignon frequently used the term "commission" to describe her belief that she was directly authorized and sent by God to reform Christianity.</note>, acts on all sides to make it odious to the souls who might receive and follow it. If he did not have this as his goal, why would he interfere in my affairs, which do not concern him at all? *Satan* must surely have raised him up in the esteem of men in order to thwart the designs of God: for it cannot be mere human malice to speak such great evils covertly about a person one has never known, with whom one has no need to have communi-

<summary>Bourignon argues that her accuser, Father V. L., lacks any factual basis for his attacks and is acting as an instrument of Satan to sabotage her divine mission. 8. This being assumed as truth, how can one allow, in good conscience, the aforementioned **Father V. L.** <note>referring to Father Vander Linne, a Jesuit priest and ecclesiastical official who was a primary adversary of Bourignon.</note> to attack me with slanders or to attack the truth (which is God) without any foundation or reasons? It must surely be that *Satan* has raised him up in the esteem of men so that he might thwart the designs of God. 4. She told me that she read the Bible constantly. 5. She told me that she would like to know specifically what she should do to please *God; and whether the task of teaching children was pleasing to Him?* saying that she did not do it for any gain or profit; nevertheless, she did not know how to see if she was doing well or not. I replied to her that she could know this well enough within herself: that it was not necessary to learn it from others: that if she found in her soul more humility and love of God since starting her task than she had before, that it was a sign that it was benefiting her.

8. When she saw that she gained nothing from these answers, she asked me if solitude was not good? V., she had been unable to maintain it; because the lady was very intent on attending every service of the Church; and if she missed going to the Benediction <term>Salut: A Catholic service, often in the afternoon or evening, involving the exposition of the Blessed Sacrament and singing of hymns.</term> for a single day, the lady would have been scandalized. She felt she had to go to the Benediction to please her, thus abandoning her solitude where she felt more recollected <note>Recollection (*recueillement*) is a spiritual state of inward focus and withdrawal from external distractions to commune with God.</note>; she asked me which of the two was better? To which I replied that everyone should, in this as in all other things, seek their own greatest perfection and use the means that unite them most closely to God: that he who found himself more recollected in God while at Church was obliged to go there as much as his circumstances allowed; and that he who was more recollected in his little room should do the same.

9. She told me that the said *V.* was a very headstrong spirit <note>original: *esprit fort à sa mode*—meaning someone stubborn or opinionated in her own particular way</note>, who esteemed nothing except what she herself did: that she had suffered much while living there. To which I had no reply.

She told me that I did not know him as she did: that she had associated with him much longer.

11. She told me she very much wished I would make the acquaintance of *Father V. L.* I said that I desired no new acquaintances: that I knew him well enough already. ## SIR,

<margin>1...</margin> I have addressed yours to Mr. C. <note>Likely Christian de Cort, her close collaborator.</note> it seemed good to me: I only tightened it <note>Meaning she edited the letter for brevity or focus.</note> and sent it the same day. The friends will copy the paper, and then I will send it to him. As for writing to Mr. Arnauld <term>Mr. Arnauld: Antoine Arnauld (1612–1694), a famous French theologian and a leader of the Jansenist movement.</term>, I do not find it appropriate: it is better that you do it yourself, if you are so moved: which would not be bad for temporal matters; but to have him in the light of the truths that God gives me, it seems to me it is too late; since he is rejoining the Sorbonne <term>Sorbonne: The theological faculty of the University of Paris, representing the height of institutional academic authority at the time.</term> he could not hear the Holy Spirit: there is too much noise there. This agreement <note>Referring to the "Peace of the Church" (1668), a temporary truce in the conflict between the Jansenists and the Papacy.</note> is a fine ruse of Satan, which good people do not perceive. I say of this agreement as of the peace between Spain and France <note>Likely the Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1668), which ended the War of Devolution.</note>, that it is not of God; but rather a nursery of all misfortunes: as I also see in the agreement for the Jansenists <term>Jansenists: A Catholic reform movement that emphasized original sin and the necessity of divine grace, often in conflict with the Jesuits and the Pope.</term>. I pity the good people who rejoice in it, as they also do in Hell.

2. It seems to me that you are very much led astray by your temporal affairs: it would be better that Noordstrand <note>Noordstrand: An island off the coast of North Frisia where Christian de Cort had purchased land to establish a religious community.</note> perished with everyone else, than for you to distance yourself from God; return often within yourself, and trust in no creatures <note>In 17th-century spiritual writing, "creatures" refers to any created being or worldly thing as opposed to the Creator.</note>: for

all men are liars and deceivers. Those who seem like good friends to you today will perhaps be your enemies tomorrow. Do not rejoice in prosperities, and do not be grieved by adversities: for all that has its ebb and flow, like the waters of the sea. The Devil has such power over the minds of men that he makes them change at every moment, and insinuates himself into the most perfect things like an Angel of light, to seduce the best-intentioned. He has gained forty-five and has a chase running <note>original: "gaigné quarante-cinq & a chaſſé courrante". These are terms from *jeu de paume* (an early form of tennis), suggesting the Devil is outmaneuvering his opponents and winning the match.</note> through the peace of the Jansenists <term>Jansenists: A reformist movement within the Catholic Church that emphasized original sin and divine grace, often at odds with the Jesuits and the Papacy.</term>, who will imagine they are doing the will of God when they are doing that of this enemy, who knew how to gain nothing or little by the force of the signature <note>The "signature" refers to the Formulary of Alexander VII, a document that clergy and religious figures were required to sign to condemn Jansenist teachings; many refused to sign out of conscience.</note>, or other evil actions: He comes now through peace and the hope of a general Reform, which he falsely persuades the best-intentioned people of.

3. For this reform cannot come through great and wise men; because God has decreed that it will happen through *little children and nurslings.* <note>A reference to Psalm 8:2: "Out of the mouth of babes and sucklings hast thou ordained strength."</note> These ones must fulfill His praises, and not the Pope, the Cardinals, Bishops, or Doctors <note>In this context, "Doctors" refers to Doctors of Theology—the highest academic and legal authorities in the Church.</note>. If those of the Sorbonne <term>Sorbonne: The prestigious theological faculty of the University of Paris.</term> can change the decrees of God, they will be more powerful than Him; because His good pleasure is such. Those who convert and become like little children will surely see this; and those who wish to be nursed by divine wisdom will discover it as surely as two and three make five: but these things will be hidden from the great

4. Thus, I entreat you with all my heart to adhere to God alone. All the rest is but darkness and blindness of mind. # LETTER VI.

*To the same person, who is currently persecuted and imprisoned for the truth, although under vain and false temporal <term>Temporal: Pertaining to worldly, material, or secular matters as opposed to spiritual ones.</term> pretexts, in which his persecutors also found their own temporal advantages. ### OF THE JUST. LETTER 6.

right for a great sin to believe it, even more to say it: for the salvation of your soul does not touch them like their own interests & malices <note>Bourignon often uses "malices" to refer to the active, intentional wickedness or schemes of her enemies.</note>.

3. You have treated with them to draw them to God & to the practice of an **Evangelical Life** <note>The "Evangelical Life" refers to a lifestyle strictly modeled on the teachings of the Gospels, emphasizing poverty and devotion; Bourignon believed De Cort's associates only pretended to desire this.</note>; and they have treated with you to draw to themselves as many goods as they could. It was of necessity that your goodness was overcome by their malices; because the malicious are too numerous & powerful. A single man in contempt of the goods of the world could not subsist against all the men of the world who seek their own interest: it was necessary that you be stifled & suffocated by such furious combatants: so much the more that your simplicity provided them with material when you gave to one an obligation, to another a signature, to another an inheritance, to another some sum of money; all that well fed their avarice; but as soon as they saw that you wanted to change your way of doing things, & that they could no longer deceive you, they try to exterminate you, in order to hold what they have. If they knew how to make you die, each would hold what he has; and without doubt Gorin <note>A man involved in the financial management of the Noordstrand project who later turned against De Cort.</note> would take possession of the first portions, of which he has retained the inheritance; the Fathers <note>Likely the religious authorities or creditors who held the mortgage on the Noordstrand estate.</note> would hold all the goods following their contract of sale; de Swanne <note>Another associate of De Cort involved in the accounting of the Noordstrand enterprise.</note> would never have his accounts audited <note>Original: *recoleroit*. 2. This is what alarms them against me, & not the fear that we might live poorly together: for if you were conversing with a whore, I believe no one would oppose it; on the contrary, they would excuse that, & keep it [quiet].

# The Persecutions

4. And you do not yet see this clearly enough. Your natural goodness <note>Bourignon often critiques De Cort’s "natural goodness" (bonté naturelle) as a spiritual weakness—a form of naive trust that allows worldly men to exploit him.</note> makes you still speak to these people as if they were your friends, telling them your intentions. I have nothing to say to him; if he wants to have the city as your prison, or the tower <note>Likely referring to the Jan Roodenpoortstoren or another municipal tower in Amsterdam used as a debtor's prison, or a request for "city arrest" (parole) rather than close confinement.</note>, let him do so; otherwise, let him go on his way <note>The original French "qu’il se promène" literally means "let him take a walk," used here as a dismissive idiom meaning "let him get lost" or "I am done with him."</note>. We shall try to obtain it [the city as prison], and we will also publish this cruelty <note>Bourignon frequently used her own printing press to publish pamphlets and letters exposing the perceived injustices committed against her and her followers.</note>.

5. Speak little, I pray you: even if you say indifferent words, they will take advantage of them, and they are glad in their hearts for your misfortune. Therefore, I would only say: *Grant me a suitable place where we may have the liberty to speak, before which I have nothing to say to you;* and remain firm on that, without alleging or answering anything. One must sew one's mouth shut in this dangerous time—I as well as you. For the little that I speak is already too much. Therefore, let Patin, de Swanne, Gorin, and their like go on their way: they do not wish to speak to me for any good end, no more than they do to you. Remember this well. You will find it to be true.

that he gives you the grace to ensure your actions and words do not contradict what I say about you. It is indeed the truth at its core; but accidentally you often do the opposite without realizing it.

2. The other letter will be addressed to Mr. Arnauld <note>Antoine Arnauld (1612–1694), a prominent Jansenist theologian and philosopher. This is why it is good to warn them. It is to my regret that it is so late. It seems that God's hour has not yet come, or else the Devil wishes to prevent it: for everything turns to the contrary, both for writing and also for printing: patience.....

3. Try only to hold yourself firm with God, and peaceful with your neighbor: with that, everything will go well in its time. I am extremely weary of conversing with men, desiring your deliverance so that I may remain alone with God: for I am no longer capable of dealing with anyone, seeing that I am not understood, and even less am I known. They do not know how to suffer <note>In this context, "suffer" means "to endure" or "to tolerate."</note> the spirit of Justice and Truth that God has given me: and I, I do not know how to endure their iniquities, which they sometimes believe to be virtues: and thus my soul is in continual warfare, from which I would very much like to be delivered, since it prevents me from listening to the voice of God, who speaks continually if one were always attentive.

### Of the Just. Letter 7.

4. Regarding your intention to obtain approval for my writings in your region, or in France, I am by no means opposed: for it would be a very good thing if one knew how to obtain it; but I fear not: given that evil, which holds dominion over hearts, will not allow such a good to be shared, and it also seems to me the wrong time to obtain it. As long as you are not justified <note>In this context, "justified" refers to being legally cleared of the accusations and defamation De Cort was facing.</note>, everything that comes from you will be received as suspicious in the places where you are defamed. We saw this in the person of Jesus Christ: even though everything he did and taught was good, he was found to be bad through the contempt shown by the Scribes and Priests <note>The religious authorities of the New Testament who opposed Jesus's ministry.</note>.

5. You must never do or omit to do anything simply because I say so, or because you wish to please me; that would be to make yourself a slave, where God created you free; instead, you must do or leave aside things that are good and proper for the glory of God and the salvation of souls. If I say things that do not meet these conditions, never do them, <insert>nor any others</insert> because I say so; but only because they are good in themselves. For blind belief is never worth anything.

6. We do not understand one another well: for if I say that I will not do something in such a way, or at such a time, you abandon the matter entirely: and when I say, "do this," you then do it to an extreme, or in a manner, or in the

VIII. LET-

fashion, so that the thing which is good in itself becomes bad: just as a very good meat <note>In the 17th century, "meat" often referred to food or nourishment in general.</note> does harm to him who takes it in abundance, or outside of the proper time. I think I have made you understand this before, but you do not understand it well, or do not know how to follow it. One must always ask God in what manner and at what time one can put into execution what one has known to be His will: fearing to rise too early <note>An idiom meaning to act prematurely or with impulsive haste.</note>, and making a good thing very bad.

7. If one must have so much circumspection <term>circumspection: cautious observation and prudence in one's actions</term> even in those things known to be the will of God, with all the more reason must one be circumspect in things that come from our own imagination, or from our passions. I have great difficulty making you understand the motives and the ways according to which God moves and guides me; if you do not understand them after having associated with me for so long, how could those who have known me for only a short time hear and understand me? That is why I often say that I am still alone in the world, for having found nothing that conforms to the sentiments <note>original: "sentiments"; here referring to internal spiritual perceptions or divinely inspired convictions</note> that God gives me, which I deeply regret.

## VIII. LETTER.

*To the same. That those who appear most devout are without charity, and the others are full of malice and treachery. It is better to rely on God without human means than to use the wicked to ruin oneself further.*

->SIR,<-

<margin>1.</margin> IT seems to me that the Mennonites <note>A radical Reformation Christian group; Bourignon often criticized established religious sects for what she saw as their lack of true spiritual life.</note> are the Priest and the Levite who passed by the wounded man of the Gospel, and looking at him, left him in his misery, until a Samaritan came who had pity on him, dressed his wounds, and put him in a suitable place to be healed <note>A reference to the Parable of the Good Samaritan in Luke 10:25-37.</note>. If this trial had not been made, one would not know how far the charity of the Mennonites goes. I await the will of God to present the Justification <term>Justification: a formal legal or theological defense of one's actions or beliefs</term>. I will not then delay for a moment. If you can still suffer being in prison, it is better to wait a little longer than to importune friends to get out on bail—for which they are not very willing. I believe that God wants to do everything Himself, and will move hearts in His own time. The less we act, the better it will be: for everyone has their mouth gaping wide to swallow us up at the first word we say. When an enemy is so strong, he must be conquered more by patience than by fighting him. This is why you should keep still, and speak the least

<margin>2.</margin> Our Attorney <term>Attorney (Procureur): a legal officer who handled the technical paperwork and procedures of a lawsuit, distinct from a lawyer who argued the case in court.</term> was sick on Monday, and did not appear to receive the bail from the new claimants <note>In this context, those seeking to keep De Cort in prison or seize his assets.</note>; instead, he had his clerk postpone the case until Thursday: it seems to me that we must let him do as he will: he has the memory: if he does not do it, the arrest remains void for lack of having given bail and chosen a legal residence in time. He is surely bought off by the [opposing] party; but one could not find another. The most honest man we know said that he will not serve us unless our own attorney withdraws voluntarily, and when he was asked, he said he does not want to withdraw; rather, he wants to pursue the case. I believe he takes money from the opposing party, as do the Lawyers. This is why one must rely on God alone, and not on men, who favor iniquity and contradict Justice and Truth: for in leaving a bad Attorney to take a worse one, and making an enemy of the first, is no remedy for the trouble. I hope that God will help us well without Attorney or Lawyer, who seek only to gain money, and not to help the causes entrusted to them....

# IX. LETTER.

->To the same, divine & admirable advice concerning the manner of making use of men & human means.<-

### SIR,

I would be very glad to see you disengaged from all creatures <note>In Bourignon's spiritual vocabulary, "creatures" refers to any human being or worldly thing that stands between the soul and God.</note>, in order to put your trust in God alone, who can do all things, making use of men only for as long as their goodwill lasts, and no longer, without ever leaning upon them; for they are as changeable as moons. Your goodness is of no use in dealing with the men of today. They are too <term>crafty (cauteleux): deceitful or overly cautious in a manipulative way</term> and hardly sincere. One must have the prudence of the serpent <note>A biblical reference to Matthew 10:16: "Behold, I send you forth as sheep in the midst of wolves: be ye therefore wise as serpents, and harmless as doves."</note> and a great <term>recollectedness (recoüillement): a state of inner spiritual focus and withdrawal from worldly distractions</term> in God, so as not to be deceived by them. De Swanne has plotted this business with the participants, even though he denies it. I do not know if you should entrust him with Father Jacobus’s letter, or others, seeing as he has no law other than <term>human regard (l'égard humain): acting based on worldly reputation and the opinions of others rather than divine truth</term>, as do also the majority of men—

## X. L E T T R E.

*To the same. One must not follow human prudence, but the Spirit of God. It is better to make mistakes before men by following the Spirit of justice & truth, than to make them before God by following <term>human regard (égards humains): acting based on social reputation or the opinions of others rather than divine truth</term>: because consolation & restoration follow the former; & the latter torment the conscience. Utility of sufferings.*

1. Your last letter pleases me, because it seems to me to come from a more rested spirit. The reasons you tell me regarding H. D. F. the Captain <note>Likely a reference to a specific officer or individual involved in De Cort's legal case</note>, seem solid to me: I myself judged that all these people aimed only to possess your <term>goods (biens): property, assets, or wealth</term>, and that the P. <note>Possibly a reference to a Priest or a specific official</note> would intervene there well enough if he saw the matter disposed to it: but I do not for that reason cease to believe, that it would be very good to declare the pretensions of H. D. to all those who might blame you for not believing his counsel: because that would justify your proceeding, even before the President <note>A presiding judge or high-ranking legal administrator</note>, who would never wish to blame the reasons you would allege as to why you desire to re-enter into your goods, before selling or mortgaging them.

These are all things that do not concern them; but are apparently provoked by others.

Niewkerke told Daniel that he will certainly prevent you from going to <term>Noordstrand: a North Sea island where de Cort and Bourignon attempted to establish a religious community</term>, and me also; and previously Verschagen and the others told me that if I were in Noordstrand, they would soon kill me. So that I must remain in prison as well as you, until God shows otherwise.

This **JUSTIFICATION** <note>Likely referring to a published defense or a specific theological treatise written to answer their accusers</note> is making a great noise among the Priests. I do not know what it is doing among the <term>Magistrat: the local civil governing body</term>; because we do not have a faithful man [to tell us]....

# The Persecutions

...that the Mother <note>Bourignon often referred to herself in the third person as "the Mother" to signify her spiritual maternity over her followers</note>, abandoned by everyone, amidst a thousand dangers and perils, as if in the Hospital <note>This likely refers to the Hospital of Our Lady of the Seven Sorrows in Lille, which Bourignon managed and where she faced significant opposition</note>: nothing remains now but this total abandonment, devoid of any resources. The Devil no longer has a hold on you except through haste, excess, and vehemence. Persecution is not carried out for debts, nor for <term>Noordstrand: a North Sea island where de Cort and Bourignon attempted to establish a religious community</term>, nor for Religion, but rather to hinder God's designs: all these other things are merely the materials that serve this hindrance. Therefore, be consoled to endure such persecution for such justice. God will prevail in His time. Place yourself in His arms like a newborn child, and He will surely guide you safely to harbor.

June 21, 1669.

But I find it painful to pay such excessive salaries. I gave fifteen **shillings** <note>original: "schellings," a currency used in the Low Countries and Northern Germany</note> for the attestation of Daniel, and three **florins** <note>original: "florins," a standard gold or silver coin of the period</note> for the copy of the resolutions of the Participants: and likewise for all the rest. I still have enough; but I pay with regret for things that are not justly owed.

.... 3. What I can do alone goes well: but when another must contribute to it, I do not know how to escape it: the devil has power over all spirits: he hinders me directly or indirectly: some do it out of malice; others out of a lack of power: it is like a deluge of evils on all sides that opposes what is good. Our **Advocate** <note>original: "Avocat" — her legal counsel</note> has left. He says that it is an affair of the devil, and that there is nothing more to be done. He is a good man; but not subtle enough for the malice of the present time....

4. That same night, a fire broke out near the place where I was staying: which made me change houses: for around two o'clock people were crying "Fire!", and upon waking I saw the flames against the **glass windows** <term>veriéres: large, often leaded glass windows or windowpanes</term> of my room. I did not think I had enough time to withdraw, but the fire was extinguished shortly thereafter: nevertheless, I no longer dared to sleep there, and thus I went away in the middle of the night.

5. There are only perils everywhere: That is why one must not wish so much to be rid of those in which one finds oneself, fearing that in leaving

...of them, one enters into even greater ones; it is better to remain faithful in those that come upon us, than to bring others upon ourselves by our own will. It seems to me that you would do well to remain a little longer where you are, without changing from one danger to another. If it had been possible to have the **tower** <note>original: "la tour" — likely a reference to a specific local prison or a more secure part of a fortification</note> as a prison, I petitioned for it with all my might. As much **bail** <note>original: "caution"</note> was required there as to stay in my own lodgings. Until now, I have not known how to find any means to relieve you other than what I have done. That is why it is better to remain in a **necessary peril** <note>A trial that is unavoidable or ordained by providence</note> than in one chosen voluntarily; because God then binds himself to preserve and help you. Where human help cannot reach, it is then that He shows Himself to be our true help—and not nearly so well before, when human means can protect us. God performs no **miracles** <note>Bourignon often argued that God does not intervene supernaturally when natural or human solutions are still available; His "miracles" are reserved for moments of total human helplessness</note>; but indeed, when we are abandoned by all human help, He comes with His divine aid, and shows that He is all-powerful.

6. Therefore, I would wish that you take every kind of measure in the place where you are for your health and nourishment, without waiting until you are out, given that one does not know when you will be able to leave. It seems to me that you would do very well to allow yourself to **sweat** <note>original: "suer" — in 17th-century medicine, "sweating" was a standard treatment used to purge the body of toxins or "ill humors"</note> when the sweat occurs; provided that you do not leave the state of sweating all at once, you can remain in it almost always. This would cause the **bad hu-** <note>continued on the next page as "humeurs" (humors)</note>

...be conscientious of this. You have never yet known the wickedness <note>original: *malice*. In this context, it refers to a deep, intentional evil or corruption of character.</note> of men as you will discover it through this business, after which you will no longer doubt that malice has reached the highest degree, and that one must guard oneself more from men than from wild beasts; because they cannot do as much harm to our souls and our bodies as wicked men do, who are today so multiplied that one knows almost no others in our time. *Jesus Christ* could well call it, *dangerous times*: for the dangers of today are almost inevitable: if one thinks to withdraw from the peril of fire, one falls into that of water; and upon leaving the water, one is bitten by the serpent: so that one almost does not know how to speak, eat, or walk without perils: For there is no more faith among men, and even less charity, nor any fidelity at all. The just person walks between two squadrons <note>original: *escadrons*. Those who do not experience this have not arrived at the Spirit of justice and truth, and have no inner eye: they are blind people, who think they are in safety in the middle of dangers.

5. Let us not be of that number: since God has opened our eyes, let us recognize the wickedness of men, and no longer trust in them; for they would deceive us under very feigned pretexts. Let us put our trust in God alone, and suffer with patience what men will do to us.

### Concerning the Just. Letter 15.

...I <note>Continuing from the previous page: "It pains me when you send me extraordinary letters [that] I..."</note> cannot do without inner recollection <note>original: *recœüillement*. In mystical tradition, this refers to a state of quiet, inward focus and prayerful concentration.</note>: and when these letters arrive, they require attention, which cannot be in two places at once: thus I lose the first conceptions of the work that I have undertaken when you send me letters at that same time, and I cannot find them again except through a fresh state of recollection: which does not always happen before the post departs. I have much difficulty making you understand my ways; because you often forget them. I know well that you cannot prevent the agitation of your spirit from working within you; but you should not follow it so promptly; rather, offer to God the thoughts that come to you, and wait for what He shall ordain.

3. As for the place on the tower, heretofore every effort has been made with the Magistrate <note>The local civil authorities or city council members.</note>, and it could not be obtained except with the same surety <note>A legal guarantee or bail.</note> as for having the freedom of the city. In this case, my lodging is better than the tower; the <term>Rasp-huys</term> <note>A Dutch term for a house of correction, specifically a prison where inmates were tasked with rasping brazilwood into powder for dye. Bourignon lived in Amsterdam for a time.</note> is not very honorable, and there are wicked scoundrels there; but there is discipline: when they do something inappropriate, they are beaten like dogs. Honor and cost are small things; if you desire to go there, you should not refrain from it for those reasons, if there were assurance that you would fare better there. If you could find an expedient to be at peace where you are, it would be better to delay a little longer: I hope there will soon be a change. Use every possible means to remain in peace: do not spare...

# The Persecutions

...of money <note>Continuing from the previous page: "Honor and cost are small things; if you have need] of money..."</note>: one only has it to use it when needed. I would only like to know when it is necessary to send you some. Use this liberty when needed, or you will cause me displeasure.

4. I would wish that you put your faith in God more than on sweat <note>In this context, "sweat" refers to human striving, anxiety, or physical exertion.</note>, or on the moon and the seasons <note>original: *les temps*. This likely refers to astrological timing or waiting for a "favorable time" according to worldly calendars.</note>; because He can heal you without these human means: and the desires we have for impossible things cannot come from Him. They serve only to trouble your spirit and mine. If you knew how to come to this total ABANDONMENT to God, you would have as many crowns of martyrdom as perils into which you had abandoned yourself. Men cannot avoid them: God alone must preserve you: therefore, throw yourself into His arms to live and to die; and you shall feel His paternal hand.....

5. H. M. <note>An unidentified associate of Bourignon.</note> went out again today to rouse friends to act as surety <note>A legal guarantor or bail.</note> for your person. It seems to me he will achieve nothing: for the Devil has power over all their spirits; but they must be tested, so that they may know themselves, and not believe themselves charitable when they possess no Christian charity. Tell no one "I want to leave," or, "I want to stay"; rather, leave everything to God: only make men think of your captivity; and if they have any spark of the love of God, it will be kindled enough; if they have none, we will always find it in God if we seek Him purely: for He loves us more than we love ourselves.

->God, ABANDONMENT, martyrdom, Devil, Christian charity, captivity<-

Of the Just. Letter 16.

Do not pray to your Fathers <note>Likely religious superiors or city elders.</note> nor others for any other favor than to have the city as your prison; you will not escape them: for one must not open one's heart nor intentions to one's enemies.

1669.

## XVI. LETTER.

->To the same. Exhortation and consolation in his persecutions and sufferings.<-

->SIR,<-

1. I have recommended your matter well to God... Leave all men and all means behind, and abandon yourself to God alone: for the more one thinks of finding relief, the more one sinks into confusion. Rest a little while longer, and let us see what your opponents <note>original: *parties*. In this context, it refers to the opposing parties in a legal dispute.</note> will do: they will provide enough reason to show everyone that they are not seeking payment at all.

Try to obtain this attestation from the Gentlemen of your chamber <note>Likely the judicial chamber or council overseeing his case.</note>: for he said that they will appease Gorin and the others, provided you remain in Brabant.

3. I pray you, be content to remain there

1669.

## LETTER XIX.

*To one of her close friends, informing him that while God works in the good, the Devil also works on all sides to prepare everything for persecution.*

->MY CHILD,<-

1. I have received a letter from Nicolas Henning of Flensburg <note>A city in the Duchy of Schleswig, now in northern Germany near the Danish border.</note>, who sent the money for the books that were sent to him, and he gives great thanks for the immense good they have done for his soul. He desires to have all those that are in print, and appreciates them so much that he has resolved to make war against flesh and blood <note>A biblical expression (Ephesians 6:12) referring to the struggle against human nature and worldly temptations.</note> to follow these truths, and to complete his penance <term>penance <note>In Bourignon’s context, this refers to a life of self-denial and spiritual discipline to atone for past sins and align with God's will.</note></term> in this world. At last, it seems he will become a true child of God. He invites all the brothers to come and stay in his house, or those who will come...

# LETTER XX.

*To the President of Altona, near Hamburg, complaining that the Gazetteer <note>The publisher or editor of the local newspaper</note> of said place, having published the defamations of her enemies against her, refused to publish at her request the book by which she had justified herself against their slanders.*

and of everyone, in places mainly where I am unknown, such as the entire Empire <note>The Holy Roman Empire</note> and Germany, where I have never been; there, he has depicted me in his book as if I were a whore, a witch, and a seducer of the people. To this, I found myself in conscience obliged to respond, and to show that all his allegations are false and deceitful, arising only from a hateful and very malicious passion; given that I am truly a servant of God, to whom it has pleased Him to communicate His secrets, and He has obliged me for several years to share various ones with others, as I have done through several of my writings printed since 1669, which have touched the hearts of many people.

<margin>2.</margin> But as the Devil, enemy of the human race, always tries to prevent the successful salvation of their souls, or to take away the means by which they might reach perfection, he stirs the hearts of many ill-wishers to discredit the new lights <note>Divine revelations or spiritual insights</note> that God now sends into the world through contempt and calumnies. This is what the said Berkendal and his associates have done; they did not have the patience to wait for the response I would give to their first treatise, but instead had a second one printed also in the said Altona, which they called once again, *Portrait of Antoinette Bourignon*. excuses himself, stating that he did not know that the books of the said Berkendal had been placed in his news-sheets, and that this was done without his knowledge. ## XXI. LETTER.

->*Regarding how she was obliged to keep herself hidden and withdraw elsewhere (as she did by going to Flensburg) to prevent the persecutions of her enemies.*<-

->MY CHILD,<-

1. I have seen by your letter that the house is better secured <note>original: "enferrée"; literally meaning "iron-clad" or "bolted," suggesting the installation of iron bars or locks for security</note>, and that one can now well remain there unknown. I would not wish to go elsewhere: but Mr. M. <note>likely an associate or local official who was privy to her movements</note> has told everyone that I am to go live in Nordstrand <note>an island/district on the North Sea coast of Schleswig-Holstein</note>; and therefore, they would look for me in Husum or Nordstrand first of all. That is why I judge it necessary to go elsewhere for a little while, and then return there: and if it were not for that, I would indeed take your neighbor's son as a valet, or a maid who understands livestock and dairy; but as soon as there is a stranger in the house, I must stay out of it, or be discovered.....

2. If that cannot be done, I will have patience as in other things: for I cannot force the impossible, and I do not have the hearts of men in my power, and I can do nothing with them except what they wish: seeing that God constrains no one, I also do not wish to constrain them; but I am troubled being near them, seeing them do things against His divine will. This becomes unbearable to me, and I wish for solitude,

I would very much like for you to make an inventory of everything that is in Nordstrand <note>an island on the North Sea coast where Bourignon had purchased property to establish a community</note>, so that everything there may be kept in good order, without confusion or letting anything go to waste, as God always teaches me, saying, *I am a God of order, and not of confusion: Regulate all things well, and let nothing perish*. This is what I cannot yet get my children to observe, who are accustomed to having abundance, and to taking the most beautiful and best when they can have it, just as cows seek the best grass in a meadow: while God tells me: *He who contents himself with the least, will follow Jesus Christ more closely*. If the children understood this sentence well, I would not so often have occasion to reprove them; but they always forget their lesson through their old habits of corrupt nature <note>A theological concept referring to the state of humanity after the Fall, inclined toward selfishness and material desires</note>, which always seeks the best, and the first place, even though Jesus Christ advises taking the last, *and renouncing one's own will*. I would dearly love to live among regenerated <note>individuals who have undergone a spiritual rebirth or transformation</note> persons, and no longer among

Farewell.

This October 1673.

## XXII. LETTER.

### *The Beginning of her Persecution in Flensburg,*

->MY CHILDREN,<-

I believe you have heard enough of my safe arrival here through J. Æ. <note>Likely an associate of Bourignon, possibly Johann Ægidius.</note> and also how the friends have welcomed me: but two days after his departure, the wind changed, when Hening <note>Christian Hennings, a local supporter who hosted Bourignon.</note> told his wife that it was I. All his relatives rose up against him; and his wife’s mother said that she would poison her daughter if she knew she would adopt my views as her husband has done: and the next day she came with a knife to kill her daughter—that is, the wife of the said Hening: so that I was forced to leave his lodgings immediately. And * * <note>The asterisks in the original text represent a name that was withheld or suppressed to protect the individual’s identity.</note> tried to have me in his lodgings. But the said mother-in-law of Hening threatened him so much, saying she would rouse the entire Consistory <term>A governing body of the church, usually tasked with maintaining orthodoxy and social order</term> against him if he should keep me in his house: Consequently, I have found a separate place in keeping with my poverty. We have two rooms, and I am very happy to be there. Address your letters there, so that the friends are not troubled with bringing them to me. Address them to Marie Pieters <note>An alias used by Antoinette Bourignon to avoid detection by authorities.</note> in care of Marguerite Sattelmacker—that is to say, "Saddle-maker"—on Angelburg Street, in Flensburg. I will wait for news from you to know how everything is going over there; having
