# XXIV. LETTER.

### Concerning the continuation of the same persecution at Flensburg.

->MY CHILDREN,<-

1. I cannot refrain from writing what is happening here: the preachers <term>original: "Prédicans"; referring to the established Lutheran clergy</term> are in a state of constant alarm. They tell their students that I am a witch <note>Bourignon was frequently accused of witchcraft and heresy by religious authorities who feared her influence and her claims of direct divine inspiration</note>, and that no one should give me lodging. The day before yesterday, two men came here to ask for Mrs. H.: we suspect it was R. J. and M. She was not at home, but the girl who spoke to them said that one was quite tall, and the other shorter and stouter, and that they were two Mennonites <note>A radical Protestant sect; Bourignon's relationship with them was often strained despite sharing some "inner light" theology</note> judging by their clothes. They said they would return, which they did not do; but they said at their own lodgings that I am certainly here in the same house as Mrs. H., and that I must flee because I am banished from Husum and Friedrichstadt <note>Towns in the Duchy of Schleswig from which Bourignon had previously been expelled</note>: that I must remain under the jurisdiction of Denmark <note>At this time, Flensburg was under the rule of the Danish King</note>: along with many other insults. The man who heard them speak came to our house to ask to speak with the woman [of the house]; as she was unwell, she had him come to her bedside, where the man said to her: *Do you know well what kind of people you have in your house? They say they are witches; and one person told me he would gladly give a ton of gold if someone delivered to him this Lady who writes books:* <note>Bourignon's prolific writing and publishing were the primary source of her notoriety and the "proof" used against her by the clergy</note>

## LETTER XXV.

->*To Major General van der Wijk,*<-
->*beseeching him to obtain from His Highness the favor*<-
->*of not being condemned before having been*<-
->*heard in her own defense.*<-

### MY LORD,

<margin>1.</margin> I was gladdened to see by your letter that there are still people in the world who wish to uphold Justice and help the oppressed; because such individuals are very rare to find in these times. For I also say the same thing, and never wish to uphold anything bad.

2. Do not resist evil where you see that it will gain nothing, but always testify that you desire to discover evil in order to avoid it, and good in order to follow it—which is what these books teach. And if there is anything else therein, it is for me to justify it, and not for others.

3. The drinkers and soldiers in the taverns swear that the Devil may take them if there is anything bad in there. This is perhaps the very best way that God has foreseen to make His truths known to men; for thousands of people will discover them through this persecution who would never have had knowledge of them without this trouble and this destruction. Let us only remain faithful to God, and He will make everything turn out for the best.

# MY CHILDREN,

1. I am not surprised that the <term>Major General<note>A high-ranking military officer serving as a protector or intermediary for the author.</note></term> has grown somewhat cold in his hope of helping us, because he stands alone in supporting our cause, and even those who claimed to be our friends are turning to the side of our opponents, because that party has more power over them than the Truth of God. Human considerations, self-interest, and the desire of this Secretary to please men cause him to resist the good impulses he previously had from reading my writings. For the perfection of his soul, I wish he had never entered into that office; since high stations usually change hearts, and greed corrupts morals. I want to hope that God will yet open his eyes, and that His Truth will set him free one day.

2. I would very much like you to give my regards to the Major General (and to the Lady his wife) <note>original: "Madame sa Compagne"</note>, asking him to let me know what <term>His Serene Highness<note>A title for a reigning prince or high-ranking member of the nobility.</note></term> said regarding the offer I made to no longer print or distribute my books without his express consent; and whether he does not think that my accusers should be revealed to me, along with the crimes of which they have accused me, so that I may defend myself against them. Ask specifically for advice, to know if I should still request this through a <term>petition<note>original: "supplicat" — a formal written request or legal prayer addressed to a sovereign or a court of law.</note></term> to the Court, or if the Court will grant it to me

However, I wish to follow his advice; and I know that he will have to suffer because of my enemies, who resist the Truth, and that they will try to win our faithful and only friend over to their side. But I hope that God will strengthen him, and will send him, through this task, new insights <note>original: "nouvelles lumiéres" — spiritual illuminations or clarity</note>, a greater desire to unite with Him, and that he will further recognize the malice of men, so as to withdraw from them even more.

3. It is a pity that this man has not read my writings for a long time. He would be even stronger to resist all the slanders <note>original: "médisances"</note> that people tell him about me, by which he is sometimes weakened; but his foundation remains good, and all would be well if he had as much power as he has will. I hope that God will come to his assistance in His own time. Meanwhile, live in peace and patience, until it pleases His Goodness to deliver us from so many enemies, who now assail us from every side. It is the time to sow in labor, in the hope that we shall reap with joy, and that through patience we shall possess *our souls* <note>A reference to Luke 21:19: "In your patience possess ye your souls."</note>. In this wish, I remain the one whom you know.

This first day of April, 1674.

## XXVIII. LETTER.

*She proposes to flee to Hamburg disguised as a servant to avoid her persecutors and to try to have her property restored. That prosperity is more dangerous than adversity. Divine advice for conduct. One must rejoice in being persecuted and slandered for the sake of the truth.*

### MY CHILDREN,

<margin>1.</margin>I find your advice to go in person to Hamburg very good for many reasons, if it could be done secretly. For I believe that the person who is there would act more out of human regard or the hope of rewards <note>original: "reconnoissances" — this likely refers to legal acknowledgments of debt or gratitude for services rendered</note> than for the sake of Justice. But I do not see clearly the means of remaining unknown there; and if I am discovered, I shall be sought by both the good and the wicked: by the former for a good end, and by the others for an evil one. In such a way, I would not know how to escape the situation well. If Madame the Major <note>The wife of the Major General mentioned in the previous letter</note> had need of a chambermaid <note>original: "femme de chambre"</note> to serve her on her journey, and if I could be covered by this cloak <note>A metaphor for a disguise or a protective social status</note>, I would indeed dare to venture there, because my face is known to no one in that place. Propose this case to her; and if any difficulty is found in it, I will content myself with writing to the person in Hamburg. He knows my handwriting better than my person, which he has also never seen. For I always have a reluctance to speak to such people.

2. In such a way, all these things can still turn out for a good end, without those who are the cause of all the evil being held guilty for it, except before God.

3. It only remains for us to pray well to God, so that his holy will may be done in us, and that he may give us patience if he wishes us to remain in infamy; and that he also give us true humility and perseverance in his love if he makes us prosper over our enemies; seeing that prosperity is often more dangerous to our souls than adversity, for people who have not yet overcome the corruption of their natures: which you have not yet done. And it would surely happen that you would forget your nothingness sooner after the success of our affairs than you did during the time of the persecutions.

> *Blessed are those who endure persecution for the sake of Righteousness.* <note>original: "Bienheureux sont ceux qui endurent persecution pour la Justice." A reference to the Beatitudes, Matthew 5:10.</note>

## LETTER XXIX.

*To Major General Van der Wyck and his wife, giving them courage, in their greatest persecutions, to embrace the cause of the persecuted Jesus Christ, rather than that of the world and its glory. She attempts to have herself reinstated. Description of her friends. Flight from the world.*

<margin>1.</margin>

that it is better to suffer and be persecuted with Jesus Christ for a time, than to reign in this world and be cast into death in Hell.

<margin>2.</margin> I am writing this to the person in Hamburg, which you may read and then return to my brothers; for it is better that I send it to my friend in Hamburg so that you may be more free to claim ignorance of its contents in case the individual should ask you about it. I make no particular demands of him, knowing well that he will obtain everything for me if he wishes to be reconciled with me; because he and his seed <note>original: "femence" — meaning his lineage or followers</note> are the sole cause of all my persecutions. If they become my friends, all will go well; but if they do not wish to do so, there is nothing to hope for. I will have to go toward the *Turks*, who (it seems to me) will be the sword that cuts off the head of this Great Harlot <note>original: "Grande Paillarde" — a derogatory term for the "Whore of Babylon" from the Book of Revelation, used here to criticize the corruption within the established Christian Church</note> of Christendom; which, being the true Bride of God, has now played the harlot with all the Kings of the earth.

<margin>3.</margin> I have seen the history of *Burchardus* <note>likely Johannes Burchardus (c. 1450–1506), a papal official whose diaries famously detailed the scandals and corruption of the Renaissance Papacy</note>, which seems to me to be the image of the great Philistine Goliath, for which only a small stone will be needed to strike down this great body and slay it with its own sword. Tell me if I need to ask His Highness permission to respond to him, or if I may do it on my own.

<margin>4.</margin> I also understand that you have proposed to go to *Noordstrand* <note>an island off the coast of North Frisia where Bourignon intended to establish a religious community</note>: and when that happens, if our brothers' house in *Husum* can serve as your lodging, you may freely take it as your own,

<meta>catchword: own,</meta>

<summary>Bourignon critiques the corruption of Christendom using apocalyptic imagery and discusses her potential relocation and the housing of her followers in Husum. She views historical accounts of Church scandals as a sign that the current religious establishment will eventually be overthrown.</summary>

# of the Just. Letter 29.

...yours, and also that of Noordstrant <note>Nordstrand: an island in North Frisia where Antoinette Bourignon attempted to establish a spiritual community of "true Christians"</note>; but you will not find there the comforts prepared according to the ways of the world, but rather a Christian simplicity. For in Noordstrant I have only a poor peasant’s house, in which I myself have never yet been. There is one of our brothers who has lived there alone all winter with the animals. He would accommodate you well with everything he has, if you are willing to be content with it. He has milk, butter, eggs, chickens, lambs, and pigs, which you can have slaughtered just like your own; for all the possessions of true Christians must be held in common among them <note>The "community of goods" was a core principle for Bourignon, modeling her community on the early Apostolic Church described in the Book of Acts</note>.

<margin>5.</margin> I can certainly assure you of the simple sincerity of this brother, for he is a child of God. He is an honest merchant from Hamburg who gave up his business as soon as he gained knowledge of my writings. He saw through them that he was not a true Christian, and he came to Sleswick <note>original: "Sleswick" — Schleswig, a city in Northern Germany where Bourignon lived for a time under the protection of the Duke</note> while I was there to propose his plans to leave the world, as he has since done. After he had returned to Hamburg, he wrote to our brothers that he had received more contentment in his soul from his journey to Sleswick than if the Duke <note>Christian Albrecht, Duke of Holstein-Gottorp (1641–1694), who initially granted Bourignon protection and a printing permit</note> had made him a gift of half his duchy. He has persevered so constantly in his resolution to leave the world and become a disciple of Jesus Christ that he has let his wife go her own way apart from him, because she did not want to follow him in this design. They have made an amicable division of their property, and are thus—

<summary>Bourignon describes the humble, communal life on the island of Nordstrand and highlights the devotion of a former Hamburg merchant who abandoned his wealth and family to join her movement.</summary>

# The Persecutions

...so separated by mutual consent. He brought his share to us, which I have not yet been willing to receive, until he has well tested whether the lowliness of Jesus Christ <note>Bourignon uses the term "lowliness" (original: *la bassesse*) to describe the state of voluntary poverty and humility modeled by Jesus</note> will be pleasing to him; and he has since shown that it is. For he is so content in this situation that he blesses God for it continually, and also receives particular graces from Him since he has been in Nordstrand. And although he has had very little of my conversation <note>In the 17th century, "conversation" often meant social interaction or company rather than just speaking</note>, he occupies himself by reading my books, and works like a servant, tending to himself and also to our animals, without wanting to take a manservant or maidservant to help him, as I had permitted him to do. And he writes that he would like to have this occupation and this solitude for all the days of his life. He no longer even writes to his wife, seeing that he does not know how to win her over to follow him in his good resolution, although he has performed all possible duties toward her. He now commends her to God and puts her out of his memory; and she keeps a shop in Hamburg, so as to earn money according to her inclination, although she has no need of it, nor had they any children together for whom she might work.

<margin>6.</margin> You will also find among our other brothers similar resolutions to be disciples of Jesus Christ at whatever cost; and all those who follow me have left the world for these ends: their businesses and offices, their country—one his wife, another his children, another his brothers...

### On the Just. Letter 29.

& sisters, who were the things they loved the most; because *Jesus Christ said, that he who does not leave all these things for his Name is not worthy of him*: They have not yet arrived at **Evangelical perfection** <note>A state of spiritual maturity where a person lives fully according to the teachings of the Gospels</note>, nor totally at the **rebirth of the Spirit of Jesus Christ**; for one cannot pull up all at once the old roots of the corruption of our nature; instead they strive to arrive there little by little, because several are of great age and have grown old in bad habits, being accustomed to living in abundance, of being well served and treated; whereas they now learn to be sober, **poor in spirit**, and to serve themselves, as *Jesus Christ* said, *that he had not come to be served, but to serve*. 

You will find, finally, all our brothers full of good and sincere desires, some like **true Israelites**, in whom there is no guile <note>original: "malice"; a biblical reference to John 1:47, describing a person of total sincerity and lack of deceit</note>; but they are not trained in the **maxims of the world** <note>The social rules, cleverness, and cynical wisdom used to succeed in secular society</note>, and seem incapable even in the affairs of temporal and necessary things: for there is not one among them all who knows **Latin** <note>In the 17th century, Latin was the language of the educated elite and the institutional Church; lack of Latin signaled a "simple" or "unlearned" status</note> well, and I also do not seek men **learned in letters**, but rather men zealous for the **glory of God**, and for the **salvation of souls**, as were the **Apostles** or the **early Christians**, who were more pleasing to *Jesus Christ* by their simple submission to the **Gospel Doctrine** <term>Gospel Doctrine: The fundamental moral and spiritual teachings found in the New Testament</term>, than the Great and Wise of his time, with their studies and Doctrines: seeing that he chose for his Apostles simple and

7. There are still many people who write to me daily, offering to join us: but I cannot receive anyone, as I have no fixed place, and am not authorized to be able to remain in peace while doing good. But if God sets us right through your mediation, I would think that he wants you also to leave the world, and become a soldier of the **Christian militia** <note>original: "milice Chrétienne"; a common 17th-century metaphor for the spiritual struggle of a devout life, often called "spiritual warfare"</note>, with as much fidelity as you have been in the **temporal militia** <note>The secular military; Bourignon is likely writing to a soldier or officer, urging them to trade their earthly career for a religious one</note>, in which you should remain only so long as you can maintain justice, and sustain and help the oppressed: in such a case one must always prefer **Charity** <note>In this context, Charity refers to the active, selfless love of one's neighbor</note> toward one's neighbor over one's own rest and contentment; but this case ceasing, it is better to flee the world than to fight it, since it is too powerful, and it would pervert the good sooner than the good would convert it in a time so dangerous and dark, where almost no one knows where they are walking. I wish you a good journey, and a safe return, hoping for every kind of good success, I remain

# Of the Just. Letter 30.

P. S. One can entrust all our secrets to H. S.; he is a faithful friend to us, and I bear good witness in my conscience to the sincerity of his soul, though I have never seen him.

### XXX. LETTER.

->*Some advice on conduct regarding several people on the subject of her persecutions.*<-

#### MY CHILDREN,

1. I am not surprised that our friend became a little cooled <note>meaning his enthusiasm or spiritual resolve weakened</note> during his journey because of so many enemies he listened to. He would have needed to have entirely overcome the world and **human considerations** <note>original: "égards humains"; referring to the preoccupation with social status or the fear of public opinion</note> so as not to let himself be discouraged by the talk of wicked men, who always strive to strike against those with an **upright heart** <note>original: "droit de cœur"</note>. It seems to me he would have done well to have my letter delivered to the President <note>likely a high-ranking judicial official or regional governor overseeing her case</note>; but since he did not do so, carry my letter to the said President yourself. When you give him the **Petition** <note>original: "Supplicat"; a formal written request or legal prayer submitted to an authority</note>, you will see his disposition well. I hope he will help us, notwithstanding that he is preoccupied by these **Preachers** <note>original: "Prédicans"; a term Bourignon often used for institutional clergy, particularly Protestant ministers, whom she viewed as lacking true spiritual life</note>; he has only listened to them as a matter of form. For at heart, he despises them all in general. If the hatred that these * * <note>The asterisks likely represent specific names of influential enemies omitted by the printer to avoid further legal trouble or scandal</note> bear toward me were removed, these Preachers would not have much credit with them. But the evil lies in the hatred they bear toward me, and not in any crimes I have committed.

One must have the **press** <note>Bourignon operated her own printing press to distribute her works without ecclesiastical censorship, a rare and provocative act for a woman at the time</note> and everything, or nothing: and I must also enter into possession of Nordstrand, or I must go among the **Turks** <note>The Ottoman Empire; radical spiritualists of this era sometimes suggested that "infidels" might be more tolerant of their message than the established European churches</note> toward Jerusalem: For I cannot remain in a place where I could not do what God commands me.

3. I must write again: For you do not yet know enough of what constitutes true Virtue, nor how much our nature has been corrupted by sin. I must make you see this in every detail quite clearly before you become true Christians. For I have no need to be near you unless it is to make known your faults, and to cultivate your souls, so that they may bear good fruit. I wish that our friends would leave the **World** <term>le Monde: in Bourignon's theology, this refers to the corrupt social, political, and religious systems that distract from a direct relationship with God</term> to be happy and content: for they still groan under its slavery, and do not know how to extricate themselves. This afflicts my soul: for I wish for them the good that I wish for myself. They must not

Of the Just. Letter 31. 101

# XXXI. LETTER.

### *That one must not act with haste, nor do what seems good to us without consulting God, especially in times of persecution and among enemies.*

->MY CHILDREN,<-

<margin>1.</margin>I received yours of the 1st of May and saw the copy of the letter written by V. D. V. <note>likely an abbreviation for a specific follower or "Votre De Vous," referring to the recipient's own letter</note> to the ***. But I had no intention that he should state what we are claiming at Gottorp <note>Gottorp Castle, the residence of the Dukes of Schleswig-Holstein-Gottorp; Bourignon was often in legal disputes with authorities there over her printing press and property</note>; rather only that he would have written to the said ** that he had written to me to offer his assistance, and that I was asking him to tell us if he was a friend of the old President who came from Hamburg to Schleswig; because the latter can help us greatly with the recommendation of some friend: and then I would have written myself to the said ** what he should ask for us from the President, in the event that he is a friend to him, and that he is willing to take the trouble to solicit on our behalf. But without knowing that, they wrote to him asking him to petition for the return of our books. This is against my intentions: for if I had wanted to pursue that, I would have had them long ago, as I believe. But I wish to settle the main matter, and to know if I can print here, or even enter into possession of Nordstrand <note>Nordstrand is an island in North Frisia where Bourignon purchased land, hoping to establish a "New Jerusalem" or a community of "true Christians" free from state-church interference</note>: For if that cannot be done, we would have to leave the country, even though

<meta>catchword: although</meta>

<summary>In this letter, Antoinette Bourignon corrects a follower's unauthorized legal petition regarding her confiscated books. She clarifies that her primary goal is not the books themselves, but securing the legal right to print her writings and occupy her land on the island of Nordstrand.</summary>

...that our books and all things would be returned to us,
I would merely be under great obligation to this

anew.

<margin>2.</margin> It is a pity that my children <note>Bourignon's spiritual followers</note> do not understand
me, and that I cannot employ them in anything
profitably: because they are still full of
their own wisdom, and do not listen for
the Spirit of God, who must lead them in all
things. For my part, I would never dare to act as
they do of my own accord, and I
often have good thoughts of my own,
to do this or that; but I would not wish to put
a single one into execution until I have
recommended it to God and learned if it would be
pleasing to Him for me to do this or that: and
when He gives me no answer, I dare not
undertake anything, fearing to do wrong.

<margin>3.</margin> But the children are bolder than their
Mother <note>Bourignon referred to herself as the "Mother" of her spiritual family</note>. This is why they so often make
great mistakes, sometimes irreparable, such
as the one where S. H. <note>Likely a specific follower, possibly a woman of status given she travels by carriage</note> came here to alight
from her carriage at the door of ** in the view of
everyone, while the neighbors are so curious
to know where I am. This will certainly give cause
to come visit her house, and discover all her

For often the will of God changes through the indisposition of men. It was certainly the will of God to make all the children of Israel enter into the promised land; but their sins and lack of readiness prevented them from ever arriving there: And it is still the same God today, who has led us out of the world to make us enter into a place of rest: but the sins and disobedience of children <note>Bourignon frequently refers to her followers as her spiritual children.</note> would indeed prevent us from ever arriving there, and we would always be thus pursued and persecuted in every place we might go. # The Persecutions.
### XXXII.

...the glory of God is always opposed; but everything that serves the glory of the Devil has the wind in its sails and prospers well. This is why it is said that regenerate persons <note>Those who have undergone a spiritual "new birth" or conversion.</note> become fools before they become wise; because he who abandons his own wisdom finds himself with nothing, since there is no true wisdom except in God. Having none of this Divine wisdom, man becomes a fool as soon as his own wisdom is taken from him, and he seems no longer capable of anything. For if he wishes to become a true Christian, he must renounce his own wisdom and wait until he has obtained divine wisdom.

<margin>8.</margin> But the depths of man's heart are so proud that he always wants to maintain his own wisdom, even though it causes him to make grievous mistakes. He does not know how to humble himself, nor how to show that he is not wise. This is what you have often done in my regard, and argued in order to make your opinions prevail over <note>Literally "float above."</note> the Light of the Holy Spirit... The Devil and the corruption of our nature always give us the fervor to do what is not good, and forgetfulness or lukewarmness to do what is good... This friend from Amsterdam seems to me like a presumptuous Collegiant <term>The Collegiants were a 17th-century Dutch religious group that rejected formal church hierarchy and emphasized individual interpretation and open discussion.</term>, who writes more to teach than to learn... This 3rd of May, 1674.

->A. B.<- <note>Signature of Antoinette Bourignon.</note>

# LETTER XXXII.

## On the contradictions and troubles she suffered from her friends in domestic matters.

->MY CHILDREN,<-

I. I do not know why you are still listening to the advice of those peasants <note>original: "païsans"; likely referring to local laborers or uneducated country-folk whose practical or worldly advice conflicted with Bourignon's instructions.</note>, who entertain you with deceits, as I have so specifically written to you previously. But it seems that you do not believe at all, even though I have much more experience in these matters than the rest of you, besides the wisdom <note>original: "sapience"; an archaic term for deep, often divine, wisdom.</note> of the Holy Spirit. I am getting tired of repeating the same thing so many times. If you do not want to believe me, do not ask me for anything more: act and ruin everything just as you please; for I cannot prevent it by force, being so far away. It is enough that I write to inform you; and if you do not want to follow my advice, do not ask for it. I see clearly that the devil wants to distract me from such a necessary work <note>Bourignon likely refers to her prolific writing and her efforts to establish a spiritual community.</note> with which I am occupied, by making me repeat the same things that I have written so distinctly. For I wrote that.... That should have remained firm, and now.... you are still waiting to do what I have long since ordered, until the time and opportunity have passed, in order to have a new order. Which I cannot understand, except by judging that the devil is troubling you

It is exactly the same regarding the pasturing of the animals <note>original: "beftes"; here referring to the community's livestock.</note>. I want them all to be put in the meadow near the house, and nowhere else; yet you keep asking me to put them elsewhere. There will be time enough to do that once they have eaten all the grass in the first meadow, without spoiling several others; seeing as the grass is growing every day now.

They should have been kept in the stable as long as possible, so that the grass could have grown more, just as I had written regarding the horses—that they always grow thin and lose their strength at the beginning of when they go to the grass <note>It was a common historical belief that early spring grass was too "watery" and lacked the nutrients of dry fodder, causing working animals to lose condition.</note>. But it seems you pay no heed to what I say, and that you think yourselves wiser by listening to these peasants: whereas you would find through experience that my advice is always best, if it were followed.

I had also written that one should give the chickens and other animals suitable places, so as to keep the rooms free for people, and you ask me again if you should do that? I am not fickle <note>original: "changeante"; Bourignon emphasizes her consistency as a leader compared to the indecisiveness of her followers.</note>, and only desire that everything proceeds in good order: and when you see something going wrong..., you must immediately apply a remedy <note>original: "reméde"; meaning a practical fix or solution.</note> as best you can, without waiting for my advice on it.

This must absolutely happen: for if this corruption is not overcome and submitted to the will of God, no one can ever be saved: and because this is such an important thing to know, I intend to compose an entire book about it, to show how the nature of man has been so corrupted by sin that he can no longer do anything without sinning. This will be the second part of *True Virtue* <note>Referring to her treatise *La Vraye Vertu*, one of her most significant spiritual guides.</note>, if God gives me life.

3. always draws us away from eternal things through the allurements of the flesh: toward which corrupt nature <note>In this context, "corrupt nature" refers to the theological belief that human beings are naturally inclined toward sin and earthly desires due to the Fall of Man.</note> is willingly inclined.

This would be very dangerous, seeing as the **Apostle** <note>Referring to St. Paul or the author of the Epistle to the Hebrews.</note> says precisely: *That there is no longer any sacrifice to offer for those who, after having known the truth,* <note>Original French: "Qu’il n’y a plus de sacrifice à offrir pour ceux, qui aprés avoir connu la verité." This refers to Hebrews 10:26, which warns that those who abandon the faith after receiving the truth face severe judgment.</note>

### Of the Just. Letter 34.

8. You had a little experience of this at Flensburg <note>Flensburg is a city in modern-day Germany, then part of the Duchy of Schleswig; it was a known area for religious dissenters to face legal trouble.</note>: for when one is imprisoned, one cannot help oneself, no matter how right one’s cause may be. This is why she must not know precisely the place where he is; nor should he remain there long: it is better to go to Husum to keep company with V. D. V., where he will be in greater safety. And as for you, I think that these married persons will take you under the pretext of being a <term>bookkeeper: "teneur des livres," a common secular job used by religious refugees as a "cover" to avoid suspicion from authorities.</term>, or some other service you could render them, or to some other good people, as I wrote to you in my previous letter. S. <note>Likely an abbreviation for a specific individual or "Votre Seigneurie" (Your Lordship/Excellency), used here as a respectful reference to a community member.</note> remains there, it is better to keep him company in Waterland, as you proposed.....

This 1st of November, 1674.

### MY REVEREND FATHER,

<margin>1.</margin>
I have received yours with the papers concerning Father *Patin’s* story <note>The original French uses "conte," which can mean a story or tale, playing against "compte" (financial account) mentioned later.</note>; which may well serve as memoirs to draw up a relevant account: But I do not believe that Father *Patin* will ever do that; for he does not seek to clarify the matter at all, but rather to muddle it further and further. He makes you believe, as well as the Fathers of *Brabant* <note>A region in the Low Countries; the "Fathers" likely refer to a specific religious order there.</note>, many things that are not true; and he speaks to them as if I wished to have the assets and not pay the debts: which is far from my thoughts. For I have never, from the beginning, sought anything other than to discover all that the late *de Cort* <note>Christian de Cort (1608–1669) was a priest who left his estate to Bourignon, sparking a massive legal battle with his creditors.</note> owed; in order to satisfy it, even from my own means, in case he had not left behind enough assets to do so; But I could not give to each person everything they asked for blindly: for that reason, I sent Mr. *Franken* and *D. L.* to *Nordstrand* <note>An island off the coast of North Frisia where de Cort had purchased land to build a "New Jerusalem."</note> with my power of attorney <term>Power of Attorney: A legal document authorizing a representative to act on someone's behalf in legal or financial matters.</term>, to settle with everyone; in order to pay them everything that was found to be legitimately owed to them.

<margin>2.</margin>
But all the Creditors, and *Patin* (who led the group) did not want to settle with me: and at the same time went to *Gottorp* <note>Gottorp Castle was the administrative seat of the Duke of Schleswig-Holstein-Gottorp, who held jurisdiction over the Nordstrand territory.</note> and each took what he could, dividing the assets like plunder, or abandoned property: Whereas, as the sole Heir, I offered to pay everything that was legitimately due; and I could do no more to prevent

so many sins and wrongful expenditures that have followed. I know well that the late *de Cort* received money from Madlle. *Vendeville*, and also that he had some returned to her: that is why there will be no difficulty in honoring everything that I can see in good conscience is owed to them, even though they have no formal bonds <term>Bonds: Written legal "obligations" or contracts proving a debt.</term> to show; seeing as the said late *de Cort* often told me that he had never given any bonds to the said *Vendeville* and the Oratory <note>The Oratory of Jesus, a Catholic society of priests.</note>, nor had he ever settled accounts with them; and that they themselves have drawn up an account according to their own whims, which *Patin* himself wrote with his own hand. And notwithstanding that, I will deny nothing of what I can see to be true: for God has given me a Spirit of Justice and Truth, to condemn myself as well as others. That is why I cannot settle accounts with *Patin*, who has a spirit of injustice and lies; and he said at *Mechelen* <note>A city in the Spanish Netherlands (modern-day Belgium) where legal and ecclesiastical matters were often adjudicated.</note> in my presence, that he knows well how to draw up accounts in such a way that his opposing party will never have anything to receive. For he studies night and day to find inventions to better deceive the one who deals with him: And as for me, I flee such people, and I do not know how you can remain with him for so long, unless he makes you have patience by force.

<meta>This page begins Letter 36. It follows Antoinette Bourignon’s ongoing dispute with Father Patin regarding the estate of Christian de Cort and claims made by the Oratory of Jesus and Mademoiselle Vendeville.</meta>

# Of the Just man. Letter 36.

3. I understand well that it is he who asks what security <term>Security: Referred to as "caution"; a financial guarantee or bond provided to ensure the fulfillment of a legal obligation.</term> I will provide, and where I will provide it: for I am certain that Mr. Coriache, Mr. Noels, and all those who know me, will rely on my simple promise more than on a security: for I am solvent, and true to my word; but *Patin* would try to quibble over every point: and if by chance I entered into an accounting with him, he would believe he could remain in possession of my goods as long as his lawsuits and quibbles lasted. That is why I will take good care not to enter into an accounting with him; for I wish to settle accounts with an upright man, who is disinterested in this matter.

4. It is a small thing you say, that the late *de Cort* did not contradict the claims that the Oratory and *Vendeville* had against him; because they never contested the account they held together: and there was no question of refuting this claim until the parties had entered into a settlement <term>Settlement: "Liquidation" in the original; the legal process of determining the final balance of debts and assets.</term>: perhaps the claim of the Oratory is due—which I do not know—but I also have claims against it, as the said late *de Cort* told you yourself, and also declared by the contract of relief <term>Contract of relief: A "contract de relevement," a legal agreement where one party is indemnified or released from certain obligations by another.</term>: confessing to owing the Oratory, while stating that they also owed him.

5. This is why it is necessary to come to a just final settlement, to know who will owe his companion, before knowing in

I cannot compel them to anything after having done before God and men all that I could to settle with them amicably. If they do not want it, I know not what to do, except that for my part I will always remain in justice, being assured that in so doing they will have more trouble bearing the wrong they do me than I will have in suffering it: for Scripture says: *that patience will heap coals of fire on the heads of the evil-doers.* <note>original: "que la patience assemblera des charbons de feu sur la teste des mal-faisants." That is why I wish that you have no part in the injustice of this affair: in this wish I remain,

->Of Your Reverence,<-

->*Most humble Servant*,<-

February 11, 1675.

## XXXVI. LETTER.

*One must leave one’s parents rather than God. Persecutions by priests to prevent A. B. from entering into possession of her inheritance. The utility of temptations.*

->MY CHILD,<-

1. I rejoiced to see by yours of the 19th of March that you are resolved to save your soul, even though your mother would remain in the deserts of the world; <note>The "deserts of the world" is a common mystical metaphor for a life focused on worldly vanity and material concerns, which Bourignon viewed as spiritually barren.</note> and that you, nevertheless, wish to advance toward the promised land. In this you act wisely: for your mother cannot save you; rather, she would surely be the cause of your damnation. For I see no sign that she will advance in virtue, as she is too attached to her corrupt nature, which she wishes to maintain and defend. She has done much harm to others through the sensitivity of this nature, <note>In this context, "sensitivity" (sensibilité) refers to an excessive preoccupation with physical comfort, emotions, and the senses, which Bourignon believed hindered spiritual growth.</note> by giving them so many reasons to show that one must care for temporal matters, <term>Temporal: Relating to worldly, physical, or financial affairs, as opposed to eternal or spiritual ones.</term> and that the body must be treated according to its infirmity and its age. And because I cannot support her covetousness and her sensuality, she murmurs against me and wants to make others believe that I have no charity. And because I would not know how to bring her out of her blindness, I leave the reins loose on her neck, <note>original: "je lui laisse la bride sur le coû." This is an idiom meaning to let someone follow their own impulses or go their own way without further interference.</note> so as to let her run wherever she wishes. And you act wisely in [re-]

<summary>Antoinette Bourignon encourages a follower to prioritize their spiritual salvation over family obligations, specifically criticizing a mother's focus on worldly comforts and physical needs over religious devotion.</summary>

having felt the operations of my words
which could not come from nature: the remembrance
of these things should make this temptation vanish...

the Spirit of Jesus Christ. Waiting until
we see each other in the flesh.

This March 15, 1675.

# LETTER XXXVII.

*Concerning her request for a Safe-conduct from*
*the King of Denmark so as to be able to subsist and act*
*freely, notwithstanding the malice of her per-*
*secutors.*

1. This is the 5th letter I am writing to you; and I have received one from you by the hand of V. D. V., and two by the hand of Sw: <note>Likely Jan Swammerdam, the famous Dutch naturalist and a follower of Bourignon.</note>. I have sent you two Petitions, and informed you of my intentions, which are, to have a safe-conduct <term>Safe-conduct (sauve-conduite): A legal document or "passport" granting a person protection and the right to travel or reside in a specific territory without being arrested or harassed.</term> from the King to go freely everywhere in his domain: And I do not know why this would be refused to us, seeing as it is granted to all those who desire to live in these parts, where all foreigners are free to go and to stay without any opposition: I know that there are Jews and Turks <note>In the 17th century, "Turks" was a common general term for Muslims.</note> under the protection of this King; and it is only to his advantage that his country be populated: why, then, should we, who are Christians, be refused a safe-conduct? The Devil would have to have great power there to prevent that. If they wish to restrict me so that I cannot publish my writings, I cannot accept that condition, until those writings have been debated and condemned for some evil: for if I accepted that condition in the safe-conduct from the Duke <note>Referring to the Duke of Holstein-Gottorp, who had previously granted her refuge under certain restrictions.</note>, it was only until those writings had been debated, and no more. For you know, that the same

### XXXVIII. LETTER.

*Regarding her need to withdraw from Holstein to Hamburg to escape the persecutions of the Priests, her persecutors, who wished to exterminate her because of the truths she announced. That the discovery of truth is accompanied by persecutions.*

->SIR,<-

1. I received yours of March 7 of this year in due time; but as I was unable to read your handwriting <note>Bourignon often struggled with the various scripts used by her correspondents across Europe and frequently employed secretaries to transcribe her own letters for clarity.</note>, I deferred responding to you until I had (through another) a good understanding of it: and fearing that you also might not be able to read my letter, I have asked one of my friends to copy it. I noticed in yours the graces that God has granted you for so long, having discovered the malice in which the world is buried; and that unless one withdraws from it, one must die from the plague that people of goodwill contract from it. This is a great and very particular favor from God, compared to

<meta>This page continues Letter 38. Antoinette Bourignon is addressing a correspondent who has found her writings, contrasting her practical spiritual approach with the theoretical teachings of other contemporary reformers like Jean de Labadie.</meta>

# The Persecutions

so many and so many people who die in the blind-
ness of these things. Still, He has
granted you more graces by having had knowledge of
my writings, which not only discover the
wickedness of the world & the sins of our own
consciences, but also the true virtue, & the
means of returning to God after having aban-
doned Him through our sins.

2. I believe that *Labadie* <note>Jean de Labadie (1610–1674) was a French mystic and former Jesuit who founded the "Labadist" sect. While Bourignon shared his desire to reform the church, she frequently criticized him for being too intellectual and not "practicing" the true death of the self.</note> has indeed awakened you from the
sleep of sin & from the vanities of the world in which you
were slumbering: but he could not show
you in practice <note>original: "par effet." Meaning through actual results or lived experience rather than just words.</note> this path that leads to the love of
God & to the hatred of ourselves, as
God has shown it to me: since he had not
himself received *this light of Truth*, which you
notice in my writings, which He grants me the grace
to follow & practice: for I teach nothing
to others except what I practice myself.
Which I do not notice at all in those who call
themselves *sent to restore Israel* <note>original: "envoyez pour rétablir Israël." A biblical metaphor often used by 17th-century reformers to describe their mission to return the Christian Church to its "primitive" or "pure" state.</note>. I notice
well that they have Virtue in Theory, by
knowing how to speak well; but no one exercises
themselves in the practice of an Evangelical Life, in the
mortification of their corrupt nature, to
revive in the Spirit of Jesus Christ.

3. That is why I have not been able to join with
the said *Labadie*, whom I knew, nor with any
others who claim to be among these sent ones; because
they all walk by another way than
that which God teaches me, & which I declare
through my writings, as God commands me to
do.

<summary>In this letter, Bourignon distinguishes her teachings from those of the reformer Jean de Labadie, arguing that while he awakened souls to sin, only her writings provide the practical path to true spiritual virtue and the "Evangelical Life."</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Jean de Labadie, spiritual practice vs theory, restoration of Israel, Evangelical Life, mortification, conversion</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues Letter 38. Antoinette Bourignon is explaining her divine mission as a messenger of Truth rather than a formal scholar, emphasizing that her insights come directly from the Holy Spirit rather than human study.</meta>

<header>### of the Just. Letter 38.</header>

do. I have never studied, and I am not sent to rebuke or correct men; but rather to bring the light of Truth to those who wish to receive it. If you are intent on doing so, you may read more of my writings, and you will always find in them more lights of truth, coming from God continually as the river flows from its source. For the time has come when this Holy Spirit promised by Jesus Christ will *teach all truth* <note>original: "enseignera toute verité." A reference to John 16:13, a key verse for mystics who believed in ongoing divine revelation.</note>; and where *the daughters and sons of those who receive it will prophesy*; and where *the Old Men will dream dreams* <note>A reference to the prophecy of Joel 2:28, often cited by 17th-century "prophetesses" like Bourignon to justify their authority to teach despite being women.</note>.

4. I await this blessed time in the midst of the thick darkness where we live at present. This is why I called my first book, *The Light Born in Darkness*, since it is born in such a dark time (in which the world is abandoned regarding the salvation of souls), that there has never been one like it since creation, where no one sees where they walk, any more than the Egyptians once did during the darkness of Egypt <note>Referring to the biblical Plague of Darkness from Exodus 10, which Bourignon uses as a metaphor for the spiritual ignorance of her contemporary world.</note>. This was the true figure <note>original: "figure." In 17th-century theology, a "figure" or "type" is a biblical event or person that prefigures a future spiritual reality.</note> of the men of today, who, thinking themselves wise, are fools and senseless regarding their eternal salvation. Which God is coming to show them by my pen. Blessed will be he who, opening the eyes of the soul, follows it.

5. I am not surprised that your relatives and various Ministers of the Churches have made you suffer, since flesh and blood do not understand what the Spirit of God is, and therefore bark at it like...

# The Persecutions.

...as dogs do to people they do not know; but he whose eyes God has opened should not worry about it, since it is written, *that flesh and blood shall not enter the Kingdom of Heaven.* <note>original: "la chair & le sang n’entreront au Royaume des Cieux." A reference to 1 Corinthians 15:50. Bourignon uses this to suggest that human nature and earthly lineage are irrelevant to spiritual truth.</note> That the Ministers of the Churches bark more than one's own relatives, I know by my own experience, since they persecute me everywhere. Some want to have me burned, others imprisoned, others banished from the country. Many have written lies, insults, and slanders against me, and they prevent me, by the authority of the Law <note>original: "Justice." In this context, it refers to the legal and judicial systems used by her opponents to silence her.</note>, from answering them. Can a greater iniquity ever be seen, than to strike a person and tie their hands so that they cannot defend themselves from their blows?

6. This clearly shows that we have arrived in the end times, where, according to the prophecy of *Jesus Christ*, *the abomination of desolation is in the Sanctuary* <note>original: "l’abomination de la desolation est dans le Sanctuaire." A reference to Matthew 24:15. Bourignon interprets the "Sanctuary" as the Church itself, now occupied by what she considers corrupt or "abominable" clergy.</note>: since the Priests do not want to acknowledge the Holy Spirit; they bark at it and would bite it like rabid dogs if it were in their power. But God always delivers me from their power, making me flee from one place to another as if I were a criminal, while I declare the truths that God makes known to me.

7. I have now fled to *Hamburg*, being unable to remain in safety in *Holstein*, because of the pursuit of these Priests, who would like to see me exterminated, though I give them no cause. I was born in the [Roman] Church... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "Ro-" indicates the next word is "Romaine" (Roman).</note>

->of the Just. Letter 38.<-

...Roman [Catholic], and in no way reprehensible in matters of Religion to the Lutheran Priests. I have come to Holstein to retire to an Island, where by various contracts the Duke has granted & often ratified the exercise of the Roman Church on the said Island, of which I am one of the Leaders, & owner of the rights of Justice <note>original: "Justice." This refers to her legal jurisdiction and administrative authority over the land.</note>, & of the tithes of the Seigneuries <note>original: "Seigneuries." These are manorial estates or lordships over which she held feudal rights and income.</note>.

8. But the Roman Priests possess all my rights & goods there against right, conscience, & reason; & the Lutheran Priests prevent me with all their strength from taking them away from them—indeed, they strive to prevent me from ever coming onto my land: And all this for no other reason than that I announce the Truth of God according to his orders. For I give offense <note>original: "scandale." In a 17th-century religious context, this means causing someone to stumble in their faith or creating a public moral outcry.</note> to no one, & do not wish to create a particular sect, attracting no one to myself, & not disputing about any Religion; finding them all to be outside the guidance of the Holy Spirit. I leave everyone in peace, & keep myself entirely solitary, without conversing with any others than those who share my sentiments: while these Priests alarm themselves against me, without even being able to say the reason why.

9. It is a clear sign that the Spirit guiding them is opposed to the Spirit of Christ, who possesses me. I believe this is that battle that St. John saw in Heaven <note>A reference to Revelation 12:7–9, which describes the war between the Archangel Michael and the dragon.</note> between the good Angels and the evil ones, & I hope that the good Angels will carry the victory, as the same vision declares; & that the time will come when these wicked ones will no longer be found—

...[sa]ved; rather, the good shall dwell together in some place where Justice will reside. The age of fifty years, which you mention to me in your letter, will not prevent you (according to the natural course of things) from seeing these blessings, seeing that I hope to see them as well, even though I am even more advanced in age than you. It is this good hope that makes me expect these things, joined with the fact that I see many means that are leading toward this. And the reading of my writings is not the least among them, since through them so many people are moved to abandon the world to become disciples of Jesus Christ, and to regain the Spirit of the Christians in the primitive Church <note>The "primitive Church" refers to the early Christian community of the Apostolic age, which Bourignon and many other 17th-century radicals believed had been corrupted by later institutional structures.</note>.

10. This is what I wish for all people of good will. For I can see no other means of salvation than to be reborn in this Spirit: since all the other means that men have invented since then are all wandering paths; and the more one advances in them, the more one goes astray and leaves the narrow path that alone leads to salvation. For Jesus Christ is the same yesterday and today, and no other Law will come to men than the Evangelical Law <note>original: "Loy Evangelique." This refers to the core teachings of the Gospels, which she viewed as the ultimate and final divine instruction.</note>, seeing that Jesus Christ came at the final hour: we must no longer wait for another Prophet, since he has told us everything that must be done and avoided to be saved. This is why I also wish to teach nothing else through all my writings except what Jesus Christ and his Apostles taught and practiced while they were on earth:

These things cannot be *heresies, slanders, or blasphemies against God*, as these Priests call them to cover up their ill will. For I cannot teach errors when I teach how to follow and imitate Jesus Christ; and I cannot blaspheme against God by teaching that one must love him with all their heart, just as he commanded.

11. But Jesus Christ says *that the Servant is not better than the Master* <note>A reference to Matthew 10:24 or John 15:20. Bourignon frequently used this scripture to justify her own social and religious marginalization.</note>; and since the Priests said *that he wanted to destroy the Law*, and *that he performed his works by the power of the Devil*, and they persecuted him and finally put him to death, one should not be surprised that the Priests of today would do the same to his Spirit as they once did to his body, if it were in their power. But I hope that God will certainly prevent them. He will purify us by their means in the fire of their persecutions: but the more gold is tested by the fire, the more radiant it is. Thus virtue will shine all the more through the persecution of these Priests, who build their own tombs <note>Likely an allusion to Matthew 23:29, where Jesus criticizes the hypocritical religious leaders for building the tombs of the prophets whom their ancestors killed.</note> while thinking they are burying God’s truth and true virtue.

12. This is why I pray you, Sir, to take good heart with me in following Jesus Christ, who has conquered the world; and he will give us the strength to conquer it also, and to carry the Victory over our visible and invisible enemies; so that by suffering in this world with him, we may also reign in his glory for all eternity.

13. If the conversations in my letters can benefit the perfection of your soul, I will not deny them to you. Physical conversation would perhaps be a more effective means of communicating the feelings that God gives us. But the distance between our residences does not allow us to speak mouth to mouth. We must see each other in Spirit, and through the understanding of our writings. And if we are united in the same will to be disciples of Jesus Christ, we will see each other in God through the elevation of the Spirit in this world, and then face to face in eternal life; there, embracing in charity, we shall joyfully sing the praises of Him who gave us here the light of truth amidst so much darkness and lies, with which men now deceive one another, and make themselves believe false truths are real and apparent virtues are true ones.

14. This is what I strive to reveal through the *Tomb of False Theology* <note>Bourignon refers to her work *Le Tombeau de la fausse théologie* (The Tomb of False Theology), published in 1669, in which she critiques institutional religion and formal scholasticism as being spiritually "dead."</note> and other treatises concerning that Theology, which you may see more of if you so desire. I am prevented from having my writings printed in Holstein; <note>Bourignon spent time in the Duchy of Holstein (modern-day northern Germany) in the 1670s, where her printing press was eventually seized due to opposition from the Lutheran clergy.</note> But God has provided other people in Holland <note>The Netherlands was a more religiously tolerant center for printing during this era, and many of Bourignon’s works were published in Amsterdam.</note> who do so in this absence. Mr. ** can certainly obtain them for you according to your wish. It is like the manna <note>A biblical reference to the "bread from heaven" provided to the Israelites in the wilderness (Exodus 16).</note> that falls new from Heaven every day for the souls who are starved for it; they can draw the necessary nourishment from it, without eating any more of those wild herbs.

And the more I claim to be sent by God, the more they wish to exterminate me.

18. I hope that God will soon put an end to these misfortunes, since evil has reached its peak, and it could not become worse than to band together against the Truth, which is God himself. You speak the truth *that one must always combat the world, since it is our enemy, and Jesus Christ assures us that we will be hated by the world if we are of Christ; who warned his disciples that the Servant is no better than the Master, and if they persecuted him, they will persecute us also.* <note>original: "le Serviteur n'est pas meilleur que le Maitre." ## LETTER XXXIX.

*Regarding how the rabble of Schleswig <note>original: "Sleeswic." Now a city in modern-day northern Germany, it was then the capital of the Duchy of Schleswig.</note>—incited by the slanders and calumnies of their Lutheran Priests—came to violently and repeatedly insult her at her lodgings, where she had left her friends.*

### MY CHILD,

1. I have seen from your letter that you are still being threatened with the house being broken into by the townspeople. If I had the opportunity, I would have sent you the Ducal Mandate <note>A "Mandat du Duc" refers to an official legal order issued by the Duke of Holstein-Gottorp, who held sovereignty over the area.</note> addressed to the Burgomasters <note>The senior city officials or mayors.</note> and the Council, to bring those ill-intentioned people to justice; but there was no carter available, and the post departs only two hours after its arrival, and the letters are often not delivered until the next day: that is why I could not send you the said Mandate sooner. You will keep a copy of it and show the original to the <term>Groot-voogt (Great Bailiff): A high-ranking administrative officer or governor representing the Duke’s authority in the region.</term>. If he wishes to send it to the Magistrate himself, so much the better; if not, you or one of the brothers, or two of you together, shall carry it. For greater certainty, it would be necessary to give—

[they are] very wrong to thus persecute us and stir up the people against us; since we do them no harm; since we attract no one, and do nothing against their Religion, nor hold an assembly for any Sect, for which they could blame us or pick a quarrel. This refers to the local clergy who viewed Bourignon as a heretical threat.</note> had not attacked me first, without doubt I would never have written anything against them: and what I have done was out of necessity <note>original: "par contrainte."</note>, seeing that they had alleged all sorts of false things against me; that I was obliged to publish the truth, and that I did not want to bring my response to light until I had specific permission from the Duke <note>Frederick III or his successor, Christian Albrecht, Dukes of Holstein-Gottorp, who granted her protection and a printing license.</note>.

# LETTER XL.

## *Concerning the persecution and betrayal by a false brother against her. Advice on behaving with caution, and without rushing into anything.*

->MY CHILDREN,<-

<margin>1.</margin>I am very sorry to see by G. W.’s letters the behavior of David Kuper. He must have had a wicked soul, cloaked in holiness <note>Bourignon often warns of "false sanctity," where individuals appear pious to gain trust within her community.</note>, to have behaved in such a way, as you see by the letters from Nordstrand <note>original: "Noord-strand." An island off the coast of North Frisia where Bourignon attempted to establish a spiritual community; it was the site of much conflict.</note>: and he did no better here than there. For when he arrived, he went to his wife’s relatives, carrying his bag and valise with the intention of staying there; but since I was concerned about him because of the money he had, I had him searched for, and after about two hours, he came to the house and brought me the money, saying that he would return to his relatives' home to sleep. To this I replied that it would not be proper to sleep elsewhere since he was one of our people, and I kept him as if by force; but he only dined one

## LETTER 41.

*As Priests persecute the truth and the one who announces it, God expands the one and protects the other. To a Lord of East Friesland <note>original: "Oostfrize." A region in the northwest of modern-day Germany, where Bourignon eventually established a printing press on the island of Nordstrand.</note>, who intended to dedicate himself to this work.*

->SIR,<-

<margin>1.</margin>
I bless God to see by your letter that he has bestowed upon you his light of truth through the Reading of my writings: it is the means which he uses now to enlighten people of goodwill, and I have often imagined that the truths contained in these writings are the **seed of the woman who shall crush the head of the Serpent** <note>A reference to Genesis 3:15. Bourignon often viewed her own teachings as the spiritual fulfillment of this prophecy, intended to defeat the "serpent" of religious corruption.</note>, because all hell is alarmed against such a restorative <term>Doctrine</term> <note>In Bourignon's context, "Doctrine" refers to her specific system of "restoration" or "inner Christianity" which emphasized direct divine inspiration over formal church structures.</note>. It must be said that he [the devil] has little time left to reign, since he torments himself so much to prevent the light of truth from coming to light through my writings, which he destroys and ruins as much as he can, preventing with all his power my writings from being printed anywhere; although I have recently received an order or a permission from God to have all my writings printed, which are still greater in number in <term>manuscript</term> <note>handwritten documents</note> than all those already printed; and I continue daily to increase them, since such is the will of God, who wants these writings to go throughout the whole world. This makes me believe that it will be in this manner that he will cause his Gospel

...there would be several presses maintained by my writings, it would be nothing but good, since I still have so many of God’s wonders hidden that it is a pity they do not come to light for the consolation of good people.

3. I am indebted to Madame the Princess <note>Likely referring to Princess Elisabeth of the Palatinate, a known correspondent and protector of Bourignon and other religious mystics like Labadie and Penn.</note> for her willingness to keep me under her Protection: but I do not know if I could be safe there, unless by remaining unknown, as I do elsewhere: For my persecutions follow me everywhere I go, and I could well say like Jesus Christ, that *I have not a stone to rest my head* <note>original: "je n’ay pas un caillou pour reposer ma tête." A paraphrasing of Matthew 8:20: "the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head."</note>, because they say I attack all sorts of Religions, while I believe I attack none; since I love goodness and truth in all such places where I find them. I seek nor pretend nothing in this world but to fulfill the will of God, who makes me understand, *That all the Religions and <term>Inventions of men</term> <note>original: "Inventions des hommes." In 17th-century spiritualist discourse, "inventions" refers to human-made dogmas or ecclesiastical structures that deviate from the simple, inner truth of God.</note> are nothing but <term>distractions</term> <note>original: "amusemens." In this context, it implies a diversion or a waste of time that leads the soul away from essential matters of salvation.</note>, and that there is only one true Church, which is the one that Jesus Christ established while being on earth, and that there is no other saving truth than the Gospel Doctrine. Everything outside of that is error and lies.*

4. And because I declare these things as God commands me, they want to kill me. It is not that they take me publicly through justice, as they did Jesus Christ, who was led before various Judges to be examined; since I have always offered to defend myself,

...even in judgment, against the false accusations of these <term>Lutheran Preachers</term> <note>In the 17th century, "Prédicans" was a term specifically used for Protestant ministers, often carrying a slightly dismissive or polemical tone in the writings of their critics.</note>: but they do not wish to hear my defenses; they have my printing press seized, they carry away my entire warehouse of books, and they have some of them burned by the executioner, without me knowing the reason, although I have often asked for it, while promising to publicly retract all the things that they can prove to me to be bad. But all these offers are not accepted; they proceed to pass sentence without wanting to hear the party involved, even though she presents herself: By which one sees that malice has reached its peak, and that the *abomination of desolation* <note>original: "l’abomination de la désolation." A reference to Matthew 24:15, signaling a time of extreme spiritual corruption or the presence of the Antichrist.</note> is now in the sanctuary; since those who claim to hold the place of God persecute His Truth in such a way, that they do not wish to tolerate it anywhere. And I must keep myself hidden in a corner that only three or four people know of. I can, however, receive your letters through the same address whenever it pleases you to write to me, and I shall answer as much as it pleases God.

5. As for sending you a person imbued with my sentiments and the Spirit that governs me, I could not do so, for being still sterile, and having fathered no true children to Christ. I have indeed received from the heavenly Father in the depths <note>original: "entrailles." Literally "entrails" or "bowels," used here metaphorically to describe the innermost, generative part of her spiritual understanding.</note> of my understanding various children of good will; but I do not yet see them reborn in the Spirit of Jesus Christ: they are still in the <term>combative life</term> <note>original: "vie combattante." In spiritual theology, this refers to the state of the "Church Militant," where the soul is still actively struggling against sin and worldly temptations before reaching spiritual peace.</note>, and strive with the Apostle to...

<meta>catchword: know</meta>

## L E T T E R. XLII.

->*To a young man, who having left her after being her servant, wanted, at the persuasion of her enemies, to force her through slander, threats & persecutions, to satisfy unjust claims that he demanded of her.*<-

1. I was quite astonished to see that you have become an unfrocked monk <note>original: "Moine défroqué." While literally meaning a monk who has left his order, Bourignon uses it here as a biting metaphor for someone who has abandoned their religious vows and spiritual discipline.</note>, whereas I previously saw you so fervent to become a Disciple of Jesus Christ: This you testified to again in your letter written from Nordstrand <note>An island off the coast of North Frisia where Bourignon attempted to establish a community of "the New Jerusalem" in the early 1670s.</note> after you had been with me in Hamburg. You protested in that letter that you would rather die than (like Lot’s wife <note>A biblical reference to Genesis 19:26; looking back symbolizes a longing for the worldly or sinful life one has supposedly left behind.</note>) look backward: And yet so soon after, by the prompting of the Devil and the bad advice of others, you flee (without saying anything to me) from your spiritual and temporal happiness. Poor blind man! Who has bewitched your spirit to make you believe in lies? You would have done much better to tame your flesh and mortify it, than to wish to give it free rein to run wherever it wants: for it is to be feared that it will eventually plunge you into hell: for after having conquered and overcome it, you now wish to make yourself its slave again for a brief satisfaction that passes so lightly, and drags so many miseries after it in this present life, and puts the soul in danger of perishing eternally.

<summary>Antoinette Bourignon rebukes a former servant and disciple who has abandoned his spiritual path to pursue worldly claims and legal threats against her. She compares his spiritual backsliding to the biblical figure of Lot's wife and warns him of the eternal danger of yielding to physical desires and bad advice.</summary>
<keywords>apostasy, temptation, Nordstrand, Hamburg, spiritual discipline, Lot's wife, mortification of the flesh</keywords>

2. Our brothers had written to me from Schleswig <note>Schleswig is a city in Northern Germany where Bourignon had a following. At the time, this region was a center for various radical Pietist and mystical sects.</note> that they were well edified by your fervor and perseverance in the Service of God: but they will now have reason to be scandalized <note>To be "scandalized" in this religious context means to be led into spiritual doubt or moral offense by the bad example of another.</note> by your conduct, in that not only have you failed to declare your temptations or take some salutary advice, but having fled from their company, you go on to despise them and speak ill of their good deeds. For you ought to have been edified to see that people who have the means to treat themselves well and live comfortably choose instead the least, and content themselves with only what is necessary in order to follow Jesus Christ, who, being Lord of all the world, chose to be poor and humble to give us an example. But you turn these things into a scandal, and wish to imply that our brothers did this out of a spirit of avarice <note>original: "esprit d'avarice." Greed or extreme stinginess.</note>. In this you act like the Jews, who were scandalized by Jesus Christ; and like the Pagans, who held him in contempt. And it is to be believed that if there had been in our company in Nordstrand <note>An island in North Frisia where Bourignon attempted to establish a spiritual community; life there was notoriously harsh and disciplined.</note> and elsewhere the pleasures of the flesh, those of taste and gluttony, you would not have left, and would have wanted to enjoy yourself there and live at ease according to your inclinations. The lack of these things has made you murmur, and even blame those who seek to mortify themselves <note>To "mortify" (mortifier) the flesh is a traditional Christian practice of self-denial or austerity intended to overcome sin and focus on the spiritual life.</note> to please God.

# 165
## of the Just. Lett. 42.

Since I have the authority to regulate this household <note>French: "ménage." In this context, it refers to the domestic and spiritual management of her religious community.</note>, you were required to submit yourself to the rules established there, or else leave if they did not please you or were not agreeable to you. For no one made you go there, nor stay a single moment against your Will: You were always free to stay or to leave at that time when you claim to have seen that your blood and sweat were being sought: *adding, so as to fill my coffers by those means.* See what extravagant talk from a passionate <note>In the 17th century, a "passionate" person was one governed by irrational emotions or anger rather than spiritual calm.</note> person! For you are not capable of generating a single penny of profit for the community, and you did nothing there but eat and drink, take care of the animals, and occasionally go to the fields with the others: things which a young boy or a poor beggar <note>French: "bribeur." One who begs for scraps or "bribes" of food.</note> could do just as well as you, while considering himself happy to have his provisions provided on such a condition. It is true that I have taken care of your soul and your body: but it was never for the sake of the work or profit you could provide me; rather, it was only for the hope I had that you wished to become a disciple of Jesus Christ, as you made me believe: for otherwise I would have hired good laborers <term>laboureurs: skilled agricultural workers</term> to work in the fields, who would have thanked me upon being paid for their days of work, for as long as I had need of them, without being obliged to feed them in idleness <note>French: "oisiveté." A state of spiritual and physical laziness often condemned by Bourignon.</note> the entire year, as you have been fed: for which I would have much to claim if I had your indiscretion <note>In this sense, "indiscretion" refers to a lack of judgment or a rude disregard for the truth.</note>.

To this you shall not induce me by your threats: For I have promised God never to employ anything that belongs to me except for His glory: And I see that all the advantages I could now grant you in your present state would only serve to make you sin further, and not to glorify God. That is why I leave you free to do wrongly as you wish, since God Himself will not prevent you from doing evil when you have the desire and the resolution for it.

It could not be for wrongdoings, as I do nothing but all the good within my power: and this banishment could only be carried out by those enemy Preachers <note>original: "Prédicans." 8. And as for me, I tell you that you are free to do so. For I truly wish for all good people to know everything we do or say; and as for the wicked, we have no concern for them, nor do we seek the glory of the world, nor the praise of men. Do boldly whatever you wish with your own people.

# 173
<header>Of the Just. Letter 42.</header>

if the entire company were to go away, they would certainly relieve me, naturally; since I have no need for myself of people, nor of lands or houses. A small corner is enough for me for all the days of my life. God has given to me for my subsistence; And I only keep Nordstrand <note>An island off the coast of North Frisia where Bourignon attempted to establish a spiritual colony in the 1670s.</note> to assist people of good will who wish to withdraw from the world to serve God well. There are lands, grains, and livestock there in abundance. Those who are not content with that should look for something better. I have no need for their labor; but they have need to work to fulfill their penance <term>pénitence: Here, physical labor is viewed as a spiritual exercise and a way to atone for past worldly lives, a common practice in Bourignon's ascetic community.</term>, and also to have their food. As for me, I have never been to Nordstrand, but I know well that there is an abundance of everything for those who seek only necessities: They even have money to buy their needs.

12. But if you were not content with that, you did well to leave: Which you could have done with more grace than fleeing without warning me, and after being away, going from place to place seeking people to persecute me, as you say you have found them in Friedrichstadt <note>A town in Schleswig-Holstein known for its religious diversity, where Bourignon faced considerable legal and social opposition.</note> and in Hamburg. It must be said that these people are hardly wiser than you, to undertake such things without foundation or any reasons. You will all together only incur ill-advised expenses <term>mauvais dépens: This refers to the legal costs and fees associated with the lawsuits the former follower was attempting to bring against the community.</term> for no purpose. Our brothers are good enough to defend themselves if you attack them: for they do not owe...

13.
