I cannot believe that you composed it; rather, it was your Preacher or those of your <term>Mennonite Religion: A group of Anabaptist Christians known for pacifism and plain living. # LETTER XLIII.

*To a Lord of East Friesland, who in the midst of his persecutions, had offered himself without reserve to God and to her to facilitate the advancement of the truth, and to protect it, as well as her person, against her persecutors who sought to exterminate her.*

### ->SIR,<-

1. I was very pleased to see your arrival in Bremen <note>Bremen was a frequent refuge for Bourignon and her followers when they were driven out of other territories.</note>, and even more so to see your perseverance in the good desire to make my writings known throughout the world: which is the precise **Holy Will of God**, though I do not know for certain by what means. He has everything in His power.

<meta>This page continues Letter XLIII, written by Antoinette Bourignon to a nobleman in East Friesland. It is enough that I warn men of good will about it. I have written about it to Holland, to Frankfurt, to Geneva, where I have some friends who love the truth; & I believe that everything will be accomplished according to God's will, despite all these Priests, who in the manner of <term>Jannes & Jambres</term> <note>In the Bible (2 Timothy 3:8), Jannes and Jambres were the Egyptian magicians who opposed Moses; Bourignon frequently compared her clerical opponents to these biblical antagonists.</note>, resist the Truth; at which one should not be surprised, seeing that this same brood put Jesus Christ to death. It is the battle of which the Revelation <note>original: "Apocalypse"</note> speaks that took place in Heaven, where the good Angel fought with the evil one, and where the latter was not the stronger. I have a promise from God that He will deliver me from all these monsters, and also that many will follow me by taking up the Evangelical Spirit again.....

2. I do not quite know how to resolve to contribute to the greed (of these booksellers) or other sins that I discover; while it must sometimes be done out of necessity and when one knows no better: & notwithstanding that one might come to an agreement with this bookseller, I would be very glad if you could have a piece printed in your quarter <note>Meaning his local region or jurisdiction.</note> which should not be common to everyone, which is not yet finished: it will be only for those who wish to become true Christians through the final mercy of God. You will certainly feel the workings of the Author <note>Likely referring to the Holy Spirit as the true author of her inspirations.</note> in your soul if you read it or correct it yourself during the printing. It is a living Gospel, not merely according to the letter, which has been so greatly abused; one must come to the practice of it, or otherwise it is nothing but deceptive hypocrisy which makes [one] descend

<sig>M 2</sig>

...insensibly [leading] souls into hell. This rare treatise is entitled, *The Renewal of the Evangelical Spirit* <note>original: "le Renouvellement de l'Esprit Evangelique." This is one of Bourignon's significant works, emphasizing a return to the lived practice of the Gospel.</note>. I also believe that it will indeed renew the Spirit of Jesus Christ in the souls of many. I wish for it to be known to all people of good will, & that it remains hidden from the wicked, since it would only serve as a greater condemnation for them.

3. I speak to you now openly & in confidence, requiring secrecy: For if my enemies knew where I am, they would try to take my life in one way or another. This is why I must remain unknown, because of their weaknesses & ignorance: for even though these <term>Priests</term> <note>Bourignon used this term broadly to describe the established religious authorities of various denominations who she felt stifled true spiritual life.</note> might have killed me, the truth will not change nonetheless. It will always remain true whatever men may do: & if God did not make it known through me, He would do so through another: so that after they had killed me, they would have gained nothing. The same God will live forever to exterminate them, & to make truth triumph over falsehood: since such is His good pleasure, & because He wishes once more to show mercy to people of good will, in spite of Hell & all the wicked. I even believe that He will yet assemble His Children, & make them rest in peace in some corner of the earth. Then we will all be able to see & know one another in the unity of the Spirit of Christ. Amen. This 23rd of March, 1677. Without name & without place because of the danger.

### LETTER XLIV.

*One must let false brothers go; do nothing out of haste; & one will experience God's help in persecution. Exhortation to suffering.*

->MY CHILD,<-

<margin>I....</margin>P. M. should not think of speaking to me since I have departed, and likewise, I have nothing to say to him. I allowed him to go to Schleswig <note>original: "Sleswic." A town in the duchy of Schleswig, now part of northern Germany, where Bourignon had various legal and social dealings.</note> to test himself; but he does not find it to his liking: he is free to go away, just as we are also free to dismiss him. But if he felt well, he should not have left in such a manner without permission, simply because of a few words spoken to him against his will. He will be forced to suffer a thousand times more in the service of the world, besides the care and labor he must undertake to earn his living. These are poor, ill-advised people who lose both their temporal and eternal happiness. If there is still time, write all of this to T., so that he may let him know, in case he is still at Nordstrand <note>original: "Noordstrand." An island on the North Sea coast where Bourignon attempted to establish a community of "true Christians" characterized by a simple, apostolic life.</note>; otherwise, one must let these <term>birds of passage</term> <note>Bourignon uses this metaphor to describe fickle followers who come and go based on their own convenience rather than spiritual commitment.</note> fly away: God will provide us with better ones. For if these people had sought only the Kingdom of Heaven, they would not turn back because of some difficulty; but as for those who seek temporal things, we have no need of them.

They deceive themselves much more than us; for when we are unburdened of them, we are at our ease, and they find themselves ill at ease. This is the reward that all those who follow their own wills must expect, who always go from bad to worse, and at the end plunge themselves into hell. . . .

## LETTER XLV.

->Protecting oneself against enemies and false brothers.<-

1. I pray to God that He comforts you both in so many tribulations: I hope that as men forsake us, God will help us, and that in patience we shall possess our souls. It is just the same with us now as it was with the workers who were rebuilding Jerusalem, who worked with one hand and with the other carried weapons to defend themselves against those who wanted to prevent them from advancing this work. <note>A biblical reference to Nehemiah 4:17-18, where the Israelites rebuilt the walls of Jerusalem while armed against attackers.</note> This is our true image; since we have as much to do in defending ourselves against these **false brothers** <note>original: "faux fréres"; refers to individuals who pretend to share the community's faith but act against its interests.</note> and other enemies as in building up **Virtue** and the **Light of God**: that is why we must take good courage, and not grow weary of working in the **Lord’s Vineyard**, resisting enemies....

2. Regarding A., I would wish that he were told as

It is a sign that this man has dedicated his body and his soul to God, since he so liberally offers all his goods to His service. He says that this island is a very good land, and that one can live there independent of all others. We shall see in the future what God will ordain for it. 4. I was very pleased that V. D. V. has such good courage for work; and so much hope of becoming a **Child of God**. He should only work in the garden for 3 hours in the morning, and 3 to 4 hours toward evening, remaining indoors in the middle of the day. We shall send him the 2nd [Part]...

## LETTER XLVI.

*To a Lord of East Friesland <note>East Friesland is a coastal region in northwestern Germany. The recipient is likely the Baron von Kniphausen mentioned on the previous page.</note>, sending him one of her writings to have it printed after he had offered and even requested to print all her manuscripts and reprint all the printed works that had been pillaged from her and which the Priests were causing to be held at Gottorp <note>Gottorp Castle was the seat of the Dukes of Schleswig-Holstein-Gottorp; it served as an administrative and judicial center.</note> (as they still do at present): She recounts to him this persecution in Holstein <note>A region in Northern Germany where Bourignon faced significant opposition from Lutheran orthodoxy.</note>, and seeks to find a way to recover the said books, without there being a need to reprint them.*

-> SIR, <-

<margin>1.</margin>Here is the rest of the treatise on *Solid Virtue* <note>original: "Solide Vertu," one of Bourignon's major works.</note> of which I had given you the beginning at our meeting. This *second Part* will enlighten the understanding of good people even more than the *First Part*, as I have added a foreword which sufficiently demonstrates the *Seduction of the Pastors* <note>original: "Seduction des Pasteurs"</note> of our time, who resist the truth: And if you find it appropriate to also provide some small Notice <note>original: "Avertissement"; in 17th-century publishing, this refers to a preface or introductory note to the reader.</note> on your part, as did the person who paid for the printing of the First Part, you may do so.

This book is also ready to be printed in Flemish, which is the Dutch language, though I do not yet know where this can best be done.

<margin>2.</margin>I hope that this light of God will spread little by little throughout the entire world, and I wish it with all my heart, since such is the will of my God. As for my person, she may well be despised and remain unknown: since that would give me more leisure to converse with God: but His light of truth must no longer remain hidden: it has been so for too long, to the detriment of the salvation of many. Good people must strive to make it known, just as the wicked strive to stifle it and discredit it. I think that God has chosen you as one of the first who will perform this holy and salutary office, since it seems to me that I have seen the fire descend from Heaven upon your offering, just as it descended upon the offering of <note>In the biblical Book of Genesis, God favored the sacrifice of Abel over his brother Cain; here, Bourignon uses the story to validate the Baron's financial and social support of her printing efforts as a divinely sanctioned act.</note> Abel. ## LETTER XLVII.

*To the same Lord. How one ought to use one’s goods: One must not be ashamed of the truth. How one may still remain in certain offices and positions. She asks for help.*

<vocab>Anthoinette Bourignon, Hamburg, charity, truth, J.C., diligence</vocab>

<summary>Bourignon concludes her plea for the recovery of her seized books, emphasizing the urgency of saving souls, and begins a new letter discussing the proper use of wealth and holding public office while following the truth.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, book confiscation, salvation, latter times, religious responsibility, Hamburg, 17th-century mysticism</keywords>

->SIR,<-

1. I have seen by your letter of the 14th/24th <note>Dates were often written this way in the 17th century to reflect both the Julian (Old Style) and Gregorian (New Style) calendars</note> of this month, that you have been restored to your estates. I pray to God that He makes you use them for His glory and for the salvation of souls, and I believe He has returned them to you for no other purpose. Be, therefore, a good steward <term>dispensateur: a person who manages or distributes property on behalf of another</term> of them: For you are only the tenant, who must give an account to his *Master*; that is why it would be better to possess nothing than to make bad use of the goods committed to us. I do not have this fear regarding your person, finding you sufficiently free from greed; and I believe also that *God* accepts your offering. One must also try not to be too liberal; but rather maintain in the distribution of goods Justice and right measure, being neither too stingy nor too liberal, doing all *with order, weight, and measure* <note>A reference to Wisdom of Solomon 11:20: "but thou hast ordered all things in measure and number and weight."</note>, according to the will of *God*.....

2. As for the printing house <note>Bourignon was deeply invested in printing as a means of spreading her spiritual message, often operating her own presses despite government opposition</note> that you wish to purchase, it will not be in vain, provided you have people well-versed in printing. I do not know if our friends from * * <note>The asterisks likely hide a specific location or group of followers to protect their identity from authorities</note> will be suitable to help you in this, since they are still like Nicodemus seeking the truth in secret, without wishing to declare it to men <note>Nicodemus was a Pharisee in the New Testament who visited Jesus by night; Bourignon uses him here as a metaphor for those who believe in her teachings but are too afraid of social or political consequences to admit it publicly</note>. It is because they have not yet well understood this passage of Scripture, where *Jesus Christ* says, *he who acknowledges me be-*

before men, I will acknowledge him before my Father <note>A reference to Matthew 10:32: "Whoever acknowledges me before others, I will also acknowledge before my Father in heaven."</note>: This is why no one should be ashamed to confess the truth after being well-convinced of it: but *the spirit is willing* & *the flesh is weak* <note>Matthew 26:41.</note>: For as long as men have not overcome human regard <term>human regard: excessive concern for social status or the opinions of others, which Bourignon viewed as a barrier to true faith</term>, they are always in fear of displeasing men, without having regard for what Jesus Christ said, *He who wants to please men is not my disciple* <note>A paraphrase of Galatians 1:10: "If I were still trying to please people, I would not be a servant of Christ."</note>. It is a pity that the maxims of men are preferred to the Doctrine of Jesus Christ because of the laxity of all Christendom. I hope however that God will make His Spirit be reborn there, & then men will hold themselves honored to publicly confess the truth, of which they will no longer be ashamed.

3. I am very glad to hear that you are advancing the printing of *Solid Virtue* <note>Original: "Solide Vertu." This refers to one of Bourignon's most famous works, "The Treatise of Solid Virtue," which outlined her mystical theology.</note>: since it is the precise will of God that all these writings come to light, we must try to advance them as much as possible. I hope that God will send Laborers for his harvest, & that all will be completed in its time, without rushing anything.

4. For this reason, many will follow Virtue when it is esteemed by persons in authority, whom one may well have as friends as long as one can please them without sinning: as I believe you do, since you do not seek these complacencies; but rather, like the Apostle <note>A reference to St. Paul and his statement in 1 Corinthians 9:22: "I have become all things to all people so that by all possible means I might save some."</note>, you make yourself all things to all men to bring all to *Christ*. In this intention, you please God more in your employments than you would by attending only to yourself while resting at ease, provided that you always keep in your soul this resolution: to do nothing more except that which will tend toward the glory of God & the salvation of souls. It is then all the same whatever we do: all things are indifferent <note>This refers to the theological concept of *adiaphora*—actions that are neither commanded nor forbidden by God, and are therefore morally neutral in themselves.</note>, provided that by them one does not soil one's soul with sins.

Therefore, always try to help her in her need; since charity can extend as much toward the rich as toward the poor, indeed sometimes more so; since the *Rich* are often in greater need than the poor, because they are in greater danger: For if a poor person does not find assistance in one place or from one person, he goes to another, & will [be...]

->of the Just. Lett. 47.<-

where I am persecuted in various ways. My friends write to me that people want to seize their assets <term>exécuter: to carry out a legal seizure of property or funds to satisfy a debt or obligation</term> for taxes <term>contributions: military levies or financial requirements imposed on residents</term> and the billeting of soldiers, from which by right I should be exempt, because the house I bought in Schleswig <note>original: "Sleesvic." Bourignon purchased a house called "L'Espérance" in this region in 1671.</note> has been exempt from all these things for many years; and I bought it for a much higher price because of the said exemption granted by the Duke <note>Duke Christian Albrecht of Holstein-Gottorp, her patron at the time.</note> and his ancestors. Meanwhile, they want to place soldiers in the house to obtain the said taxes, which I do not owe at all.

They also want to seize my property to pay for the repairs of the window panes that ill-wishers came to break out of pure malice and enmity; these individuals were sentenced to pay for these breakages, and now that the repair is finished, they refuse to pay, and the glazier threatens me daily with a seizure for this payment.

And although all these things are unjust, I have no one to uphold my rights: for the neighbors on both sides of my house have greatly encroached upon my property <term>héritage: refers to her landed estate or inheritance</term>, and continue to do so; so that little by little they might well take the whole thing if it is not remedied. This is because the Preachers <term>Prédicans: a term Bourignon used for the Protestant clergy, who often incited the local population against her mystical sect</term> incite the people to do us as much harm as they can, and no one defends me, because we are not of this world, and because I cannot pursue my rights through underhanded means. I prefer to *suffer and endure the seizure of my goods with joy than to offend God by some corrup-*

### LETTER XLVIII.

*To the same Lord of East Friesland <note>Christian Eberhard (1665–1708), the Prince of East Friesland, who was a devoted follower of Bourignon's teachings.</note>, to moderate the great Zeal with which he initially wished to publish the truth that he had just embraced in his writings. That one must yield something to human infirmity in the publication of the truth: but that one must also resolve oneself to the persecutions of men; for if one wishes to please them without opposing them, one will have no perseverance.*

#### SIR,

<margin>I ....</margin> I pray you to use discretion in this undertaking, because the enemies of truth are too powerful and subtle for

4. 5. And were it not for this, all those who read my writings would see clearly that they are in conformity with the Gospel; but when they go to consult these modern theologians; they advise the people against reading my writings, fearing that through them they might come to the knowledge of the truth. This they prevent as much as they can for their own interest; valuing it more than the salvation of souls, which they ought to love more than their own lives, if they were true Pastors.

# L E T T E R. XLIX.

*To a Friend, who was immeasurably saddened to have seen this Lady* <note>Bourignon often referred to herself in the third person as "this Lady" or "the Girl" in her published letters.</note> *leave her home to flee—without anyone knowing where—the fury of her Persecutors, the Lutheran Priests of* HAMBURG, *who two days prior had sent four armed Sergeants through the courts to her home to take her prisoner. And who continued in this design. She exhorts him to suffer in tranquility the persecutions of men, without consulting the movements of corrupted nature, but following the direction of the Holy Spirit.*

->MY CHILD,<-

<margin>I.</margin>
IT seems to me that you are too sensitive to nature <note>In Bourignon's vocabulary, "nature" or "corrupted nature" refers to human emotions, physical attachments, and ego that have not yet been purified by divine grace.</note>, by leaning too much upon my person: you should be thus attached

For this reason, one must never follow them, but rather the movements of the Holy Spirit, which always lead toward doing good; since we have these at hand, and he distributes them to us through his goodness and mercy. 5. These gentlemen have threatened to exterminate us all, but I think that human consideration <note>original: "l’égard humain." This implies a regard for public opinion, social standing, or basic human decency that might prevent the authorities from carrying out their most violent threats.</note> will hold them back....

6. All the friends here are fortified by this persecution, and W. <note>Likely a reference to one of her local supporters in Hamburg.</note> hates his priests, who act so unreasonably, who tolerate many prostitutes and wicked people, but persecute good folk.

[In Hamburg]
June 15, 1677.

# LETTER 51.

->Regarding the same persecution.<-

## MY CHILD,

1. I have clearly seen that you must gain experience before becoming wise; since simple speculation is vain, as you are beginning to discover: and I wish for you to understand this more deeply. You have done well to write to Holland roughly what has happened to us, so that they may conduct themselves accordingly.

2. I fear that this S. <note>An unidentified prospective follower or employee.</note> seeks to gain money rather than the Kingdom of heaven; since he wishes to know, above all, what employment he will have with the Baron <note>Likely Baron Christian Knorr von Rosenroth, a philosopher and diplomat who was part of Bourignon's circle of influential contacts.</note>: It is as if he were asking, how much will he gain from it? For if he sought only the Kingdom of heaven, he would simply join the company of Christians to do there whatever he is found capable of. If he knew how to well translate my writings into German, we would employ him for that... in that case he would be <term>advantageous</term><note>original: "duisible." An archaic French term meaning useful, profitable, or beneficial.</note> to us, on the condition that he does not seek to gain money, but rather to become a true Christian, and all the rest would be given to him....

3. ... I would very much like for you to change your location when you wish to confer together: For if you often go to the Church of St. Nicholas <note>The Hauptkirche St. Nikolai in Hamburg, a focal point of the city's religious life and a risky place for "sects" to gather unnoticed.</note> at the same hour, it may be noticed: and it

## LETTER LII.

### *Concerning the same persecution.*

MY CHILD,

1. Nothing further has happened in this matter except that the neighbors are spying on the house, and that the brother-in-law of ** <note>The name is omitted in the original text, likely to protect the individual’s identity or because the recipient already knew whom she meant.</note> has preached that the Gentlemen of the City <note>The city magistrates or council members of Hamburg.</note> are doing very well to exterminate heresies, as they have begun to do; that he hopes they will continue, and that for this reason God will bless them. Is that not a fine reason for a blessing? <note>Bourignon often used sharp irony to criticize the institutional church's definition of "blessing" and "heresy."</note> It seems that this Priest is the first cousin of Burchardus. <note>Johannes Burchardus (1641–1705) was a prominent Lutheran theologian in Hamburg and one of Bourignon's most vocal critics.</note> In the future, he too must be taught his lesson to make him wiser. I will inform you of whatever else transpires. The friends <note>A term Bourignon used for her circle of spiritual followers.</note> are more fervent than before. God be blessed for it.

2. H. informs me of having visited General van der Wijk, and finds him, along with his wife, so weary of the world that they no longer know how to converse with anyone, and they long for the hour when we shall gather in some place so they may come and join us. He would very much like to sell the Castle they bought in Holland, even at a great loss. It seems the Devil sees that God is disposing the hearts of many; for this reason he is so furious. I would gladly know if Mr. General B. has received my letter, and if he will help us: so that I may govern myself accordingly,

This 18th of June, 1677.

<meta>This page continues Letter 53, following the previous discussion of the persecutions in Hamburg. It is said of God that we only converse among ourselves. It cannot be out of friendship that *Burchardus* <note>Caspar Burchardus (1627–1694), a Lutheran deacon in Hamburg and a vocal critic of Bourignon.</note> sends you plants, since he has so often wished for my death. Perhaps that old woman wanted to sell you her milk in order to poison you. One must be very careful to avoid receiving anything in this manner from anyone at all. When Franque <note>Pierre Franque, one of Bourignon's loyal disciples who often managed her affairs.</note> asked *Burchardus* how he would prove by *Scripture that he was permitted to persecute me for a religious sentiment? Burchardus* replied that *Scripture forbids having correspondence with heretics; that one must not lodge them, nor eat or drink with them.* To which Franque replied: *Do exactly that with us. Leave us as we are, and do not associate with us. We are not seeking you out.*

2. In similar encounters, one should always say that we converse with no one, and that we love solitude and our inner recollection; such a statement can only serve to edify. But as for this toll-master and his colleagues, one can certainly have them as friends without visiting them. One must always say that we do not give alms to anyone except those we know, and when we know that the poor will make good use of them. Let **T.** <note>Likely Tielman, another member of her inner circle.</note> try to go to Nordstrand and Friedrichstadt <note>Nordstrand and Friedrichstadt were locations in Schleswig-Holstein known for their relative religious tolerance, where Bourignon sought to establish a community.</note> for the business I wrote about; by that time there may be some change here. It is not good for **\*\*** to come, for they are asking after him...

June 22, 1677.

## LETTER 54.

->*Concerning the same persecution, and upon her arrival*<-
->*at Lutzburg, in East Friesland.*<- <note>Lutzburg refers to Lütetsburg, located in modern-day Lower Saxony, Germany. Bourignon sought refuge there under the protection of the local nobility.</note>

MY CHILD,

<margin>1.</margin>I fear that you are not yet sufficiently convinced in your soul that it is right not to yield to evil, so as to resist it manfully: because it is easier for human nature, and also more honorable, to give to the unjust what they demand, rather than to resist them. But that is by no means a Christian perfection; rather, it is a cooperation with their greed and sins. I wish that you had had more time to discuss such matters with me, and that you had absorbed a little more of my spirit: but too much preoccupation with temporal <term>temporal: worldly or material concerns, as opposed to spiritual ones</term> things has prevented you, and caused you to lose much beneficial instruction.

2. I choose to believe that God will one day draw you out of this labyrinth for the good of your soul: perhaps God will force you to this by His scourges <note>Bourignon often interpreted political or natural disasters as divine "scourges" or punishments following the rejection of her message.</note>, which have often followed the places from which I have departed. I fear that it will happen thus to the city of HAMBURG, which seeks to kill the Prophets and stone those who are sent to her.... <note>A reference to the biblical lament over Jerusalem in Matthew 23:37.</note>

3. The Baron <note>Baron Dodo II von Knyphausen (1641–1698), a supporter who provided Bourignon with a sanctuary at his castle in Lütetsburg.</note> has given us a fine house with large gardens in front of his Castle. He has designated it to lodge foreign friends.

<meta>catchword: I</meta>

<summary>In this letter, Bourignon advises a follower against compromising with "unjust" demands and reflects on her relocation to Lütetsburg under the protection of Baron von Knyphausen. She warns that Hamburg may face divine punishment for its "persecution" of her movement.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Lütetsburg, East Friesland, Baron von Knyphausen, Hamburg, spiritual resistance, Christian perfection, divine punishment</keywords>

I will remain there for some time unknown <note>Bourignon often traveled incognito to avoid the attention of local authorities or hostile clergy.</note>. He has another one for N. A fine one in the farmyard; in short, there are so many goods here dedicated to the <term>Children of God: the name Bourignon used for her community of spiritual followers</term>, that I do not know if there will be enough people to occupy it all. The Baron says that he will not concern himself with anyone's opposition in order to increase the glory of God: that his property belongs to him, and not to his wife; his jurisdiction extends very far, even to the gates of the city of Norden: all good land, and abundant. One must not fear that the Children of God who retreat here will lack provisions: for this man is so moved and abandoned to God, that he leaves everything at my disposal. He also has a Castle an hour away from his own, which he intends for the sick and orphans, or elderly persons, with good revenues.....

4. To Mr. W., I have nothing specific to say, except to wish him every blessing, and perseverance in the truth of God, being very glad that he defends this truth as much as he can, and that these Priests will leave him in peace, since there is nothing to be gained with them; they are too biased, inveterate in their old customs. I would very much like to know the name of the brother-in-law of * * , and in which Church he is a Pastor: what he has preached about me and what he says still.... I commend you to God.

At Lutzbourg in East Friesland <note>original: "Lutzbourg en Oostfrize"</note>:
This 24th of July, 1677.

<header>The Persecutions</header>

## LETTER 55.

*On the same persecution in Hamburg, the publication of her books, and various persons.*

->MY CHILD,<-

1. .... I cannot keep those who do not embrace a Gospel life <note>Bourignon used the term "Evangelical" or "Gospel life" to describe a radical, literal adherence to the teachings of Jesus, often involving the renunciation of private property.</note>, and who seek their own interests, living in their self-love, according to what God has taught me in His word.

2. It seems that the malice of the Preachers <note>original: "Prédicans"; a term she used for the established Protestant clergy, whom she viewed as corrupt officials rather than true spiritual leaders.</note> of Hamburg has forced me to do the will of God, letting me come to this place sooner than I had thought. For they have persuaded their Magistrate to order a warrant for my arrest, sending four sergeants to the home of Mr. ** <note>likely her associate Christian de Cort</note>, where I had been staying, to take me away in broad daylight, having already sentenced me for my entire life to the *spinning house* <note>original: "in Spinhuis"; a 17th-century house of correction where women were imprisoned and forced to perform manual labor, such as spinning wool.</note>; thus beginning my trial with the execution of the sentence, based on the lies invented by their priests. They had reported to them that I had set up a printing press in said lodgings, and that I was printing wicked doctrines there to seduce their people, adding that I held conventicles <term>secret, unauthorized religious gatherings</term> or meetings there. They found all of this to be completely false and deceptive, seeing as I have never done that, nor was there any sign of a printing press. And God had allowed me to leave that place a day before these sergeants arrived,

Hamburg, Spinning House, Printing Press, Preachers, Magistrate, Conventicles

<meta>This page continues Antoinette Bourignon’s description of her escape from Hamburg. The previous page detailed the false accusations of the local clergy (the "Preachers"), who claimed she was running a secret printing press and hosting illegal religious meetings.</meta>

<header>On the Righteous. Letter 55.</header>

...so that they were left with nothing but the embarrassment of seeing that their Preachers <note>original: "Prédicans"; a term Bourignon used for the professional Protestant clergy, whom she regarded as corrupt or misguided officials.</note> are liars. And as the Devil is always mistaken in his undertakings, he had me driven out to a place where I will be able to crush his head better than I ever could have done in Hamburg, even if I had remained there all the days of my life. May God be eternally blessed!

3. God has miraculously protected us until now in various encounters. For in all our troubles, not a single paper has been lost of what God wants me to make known; and the printed books they destroyed are always recoverable, and they can also be translated into various languages, even if I were to die. If those of Hamburg had visited the house two days earlier, almost all my original manuscripts were there. God prevented this and also saved my person in time. For which I bless Him, since He wishes for me to bring them to light before I die. He will give me a little more life and liberty.

4. It is almost all the same which of my books one reads first, since all tend to teach the state of a true Christian. Whether it be under the pretext of controversies or instructive epistles, it is the same thing in substance, seeing as it all comes from the same spirit. It is only for the sake of order that it is good to read them in the sequence in which they are printed. If God had wished to bring to light the *Renewal* <note>Referring to her work *Le Renouvellement de l’Esprit Évangélique* (The Renewal of the Gospel Spirit).</note>

She asserts that all her writings, regardless of their specific topic, serve the singular goal of defining the life of a true Christian.</summary>

of the Gospel Spirit before the others, He would have inspired it in me beforehand; one must always refer to His guidance, and not set one's hand to correct His works, or to regulate and order them according to the fashion of men.

5. True brothers are, alas! so difficult to find that unless one has the spirit of discernment from God, one could easily be mistaken, and join with or trust in children of the Devil while thinking they are children of God. S. <note>Bourignon frequently used initials to refer to her disciples; this may refer to a specific member of her circle, such as the scientist Jan Swammerdam who was a follower for a time.</note> is still too trusting of everyone: he loves and knows the good, but does not know the evil hidden under the cover of good. He must have more experience, and work in quietness at his own perfection and conversion, before he can be capable of converting or perfecting others. He has a good <term>Zeal</term> <note>In 17th-century spiritual writing, "zeal" often referred to a passionate but sometimes misguided or "raw" religious energy that needed to be tempered by maturity.</note> for working in the Lord’s vineyard; but I fear he rises too early, and that he does not yet see clearly for himself where he is walking. He must become a disciple of the Holy Spirit before wishing to be a Master. It is in this sense that the Scripture says to Christians, *That no one should be called Master or Father among them.*

6. But nature always wants to teach rather than to learn, as long as it is not overcome and subjected to reason and to the Spirit of God. This is something few people understand; believing they are doing well by spreading their good sentiments to convert others: in which they do themselves harm without benefiting the...

# LETTER LVII.

*Concerning her withdrawal and that of her followers from Holstein, because of the persecutions and wrongs that had been continually done to them there.*

### MY CHILDREN,

1. I am sending you this letter for Floris <note>Floris Marchau, a devoted follower and printer for Bourignon.</note>, so that you may translate it and send it to him. I have not received your last letters because Brother Tiellens <note>likely Françoise Tiellens, a member of Bourignon's inner circle.</note> has not yet arrived here. I have the means here to have you all near me. If you could manage to lease out our house while reserving the back room, as I previously wrote to you, I would leave HOLSTEIN <note>At this time, Holstein was a duchy under Danish and imperial influence where Bourignon had attempted to establish a community on the island of Nordstrand.</note>, since we are persecuted so much there and can find no justice. We must wait for God to restore it to us and withdraw until the storm has passed. Write to Floris that he should not rush anything; he will have enough time to do everything in order. Let him inform you of the opportunities he finds to lease the lands to one person or to several, whichever would be best...

2. I fear now that we will not obtain the confirmation of the house’s exemption <note>original: "franchiſe de la maiſon." In Bourignon's mystical theology, this refers to the spiritual union of the soul with God, often associated with the approaching end of the world and the return of true Christianity.</note>; perhaps it is closer than one thinks, since God is preparing so many different people for it. The Baron’s <note>Likely referring to the Baron von Lützow, one of Bourignon's aristocratic supporters who helped her establish the community on Nordstrand.</note> wife is much better disposed since she spoke to me. She shows me every kind of friendship, for which he blesses God, and I do as well.

## LETTER LVIII.

*Regarding the fact that her friends were persecuted at Schleswig <note>original: "Sleeswik." Antoinette Bourignon had established a community and a printing press on the island of Nordstrand near Schleswig, but faced intense opposition from the local Lutheran clergy.</note> by soldiers, incited by the slanders of the Priests & the rabble. Exhortation to suffering. Advice on conduct toward the enemies of truth.*

->MY CHILD,<-

1. YOURS of January 29th is the first I have received from you since I wrote to you at your request, assuring myself that you would take nothing in bad part, whether it were bitter or sweet; to which letter I received no response from you, although it deserved one well enough. You inform me by your last of January 29th that our brothers are being beaten and driven out of my house, having been forced to abandon all our furniture there. This has undoubtedly been very harsh and difficult for them. But I hope that this will be remedied when a friend of mine is there. For otherwise, this would greatly inconvenience me. I have no money in the house, but many papers that are necessary to me, as well as all my linens and clothes, together with those of ** and ** <note>The asterisks represent names omitted in the original printing to protect the identity of Bourignon's followers from further persecution.</note> which they left in our keeping. That is why I wish to hope that God will not allow these so necessary things to be taken from us so unseasonably. I also hope that our printing press <note>Bourignon’s private press was a primary tool for spreading her "Restoration of the Gospel" message, making it a frequent target for seizure by religious authorities.</note> will be returned along with our books.

<meta>catchword: 2.... That</meta>

<summary>In this letter, Antoinette Bourignon responds to reports of her followers being violently expelled from her home in Schleswig and expresses concern over her seized property, particularly her manuscripts and printing press.</summary>
<keywords>Schleswig, religious persecution, printing press, spiritual property, Lutheran clergy, Nordstrand community</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues Letter 58, which began on the previous page. Having addressed the immediate physical danger and the loss of property in Schleswig, Antoinette Bourignon now shifts to the spiritual significance of these trials.</meta>

<header>The Persecutions</header>

2. ... What shall we do, since malice is so relentless that we can no longer resist it? We must resolve ourselves to suffering, embracing the crosses that it pleases God to send us. This is the prophecy that Jesus Christ made for his disciples, saying to them, *If they persecuted me, they will also persecute you* <note>original: "S'ils m'ont persecuté, ils vous persecuteront aussi." A reference to John 15:20.</note>, speaking of the wicked and the Pharisees, who are the priests of this time <note>Bourignon frequently compared the established clergy—both Catholic and Protestant—to the biblical Pharisees, accusing them of prioritizing ritual and law over true spiritual life.</note>.

That is why I am not shaken by anything, knowing that all these things must happen to the true disciples of Jesus Christ, who should consider themselves happy to endure persecution for the sake of Righteousness. We must take it as a sign of God's friendship when he makes us take up the cross to follow him. This is, in effect, a very honorable emblem, and an assurance that one is a disciple of Jesus Christ, provided that these crosses and persecutions are not caused by our own faults. For the worldly also have crosses to carry, and often far heavier and more burdensome than are the crosses of the Children of God, who carry their afflictions lightly through entire resignation to his holy will.

3. Oh, how happy you would be if you could overcome, as you say, those two great corrupters of your soul: *namely, cursed reason & self-will!* <note>In Bourignon’s theology, "reason" (human intellect) and "self-will" (ego) were the primary obstacles to receiving "Divine Light."</note> For these are the two things that prevent you from being a Child of God. Pray, therefore, and work to achieve this victory. We must not yet speak of storing our trunks <note>original: "coffres."</note> at C. R.’s house, nor of sending one of the let—

<meta>catchword: tres</meta>

<summary>Bourignon exhorts her followers to view their persecution as a spiritual badge of honor that aligns them with Jesus Christ, while identifying human reason and self-will as the ultimate internal enemies.</summary>

# of the Righteous. Letter 58.
## P
<meta>catchword: dent.</meta>

...ters to Burchardus. First, let the storm pass. Let us see how it will cease. You may certainly do what our brothers tell you on my behalf; for they are faithful, & will not lie to you: But when they speak of themselves, they can err & commit irreparable faults, just as I would also be myself if I followed my own natural impulses <note>Bourignon often warned against the "natural" self, which she believed was corrupted by ego and intellect, as opposed to being moved by God.</note>. But one can never fail in following the orders & movements of the Holy Spirit.

4. As for the Superintendent <note>In the Lutheran church, a "Superintendent" is a high-ranking cleric with oversight similar to a bishop.</note> Monsieur H:, you may well write to him as if from yourself what you propose to me, namely *that you know a Lord who willingly assists those who are persecuted for Righteousness.* I have even spoken of it to Her Highness of East Friesland <note>East Friesland, a territory in the northwest of modern Germany, served as a refuge for Bourignon after she was driven out of Schleswig-Holstein.</note>, with whom I am at present for her consolation. She is a Princess Regent who is willing to protect all good people.

5. I have not seen your old letter that you say you sent to me, & have also no need to see it, to be willing to believe what you tell me of it. If A. attached himself only to Jesus Christ, as he writes & advises others, he would leave his sins of debauchery <note>original: "paillardise." This likely refers to a former associate whom Bourignon now views as a hypocrite.</note> in which he is entrenched, & would strive to become a Christian, as he has often proposed by means of the truths he has read in my writings. I do not doubt that W: will resist well the hissing of this Serpent, by holding to what he has learned of the true truth. It is a marvel that the Devil has so many people who support him...

## of the Righteous. Letter 58.

[not to write to everyone; but I] would very much like you to send a copy of this to our brothers in Schleswig <note>original: "Sleesvic", referring to the town in Northern Germany where Bourignon had a following.</note> to console them, as I do not have the time to write to them at length. When you write to the Superintendent <note>A high-ranking Lutheran ecclesiastical official, equivalent to a bishop.</note>, tell him also that I love Miss M., since through him she sent me her regards. Send this enclosed [letter] as soon as possible, and let me have their reply. They must remain there until further orders. I hope there will soon be a change for the better.

8. I am not surprised that your wife is inconstant, since she follows the movements of her corrupt nature and her unregulated passions, which are united with the Devil, taking on his qualities of inconstancy. It will be enough for you to remain in the truths of God, which He has given you the grace to know. Do not abuse this grace. Your salvation is worth much more than pleasing a wife who should follow her husband in every kind of good rather than contradicting him: Furthermore, according to God's command, a wife must be submissive to her husband in things that are good or indifferent <note>Bourignon uses "indifferent" in the theological sense of "adiaphora"—matters not essential to the faith where one may obey worldly or domestic authority.</note>. It would be very desirable if your wife were inclined to follow the will of God as you desire to do; but since she does not wish to do that, see the counsel that Jesus Christ gives in a similar case, when He says:

> *that he who does not leave Father, Mother, Wife & Children for his love, is not*

I would very much like for you to send a copy of this to our brothers in Schleswig <note>A city in Northern Germany; at the time, a center for various dissenting religious groups.</note> to comfort them, as I do not have the time to write much to them. > *that he who does not leave Father, Mother, Wife & Children for his love, is not*

# 230
# The Persecutions

[chan]ge. It is much better to suffer and be afflicted for a little while in this valley of tears <note>A traditional biblical and liturgical reference (from the *Salve Regina*) to the hardships of earthly life compared to the joy of heaven.</note> than to rest here, only to remain afterward in eternal sadness. This I say to all my children and friends, advising them to choose rather **THE CROSS OF JESUS CHRIST** to follow him, than to choose the ease and rest of corrupt nature, which wishes to enjoy more than to suffer; although suffering is infallibly necessary in this world or in the one to come because of sin.

4. Man has acquired for himself pains and sufferings: it is up to him to know which time he wishes to choose for this suffering, since he cannot absolutely avoid it, it being joined with and inseparable from sin. Therefore, he is blessed who chooses the short time of this miserable life to weep and suffer, so that he may rejoice in peace for all Eternity with Jesus Christ, who through suffering thus entered into his glory—into which those who wish to rest in joy in this mortal world shall in no way enter. They surely deprive themselves of eternal rest, where one can only enter through the door, which is Jesus Christ, who says to all Christians: *Be my imitators*: so that those who have not followed him in this world through suffering cannot follow him in his joy in Eternity.

5. In this, many people will be deceived at the time of death, who believed they had a share in the Merits of Jesus Christ <note>This refers to the theological concept that Christ's sacrifice earned salvation for humanity; Bourignon argues that this "merit" is not applied automatically but requires active imitation of Christ's life.</note> without imitating him; indeed, while leading the course of their lives by a path entirely contrary...

### LETTER LX.

->*To her friends remaining in* HOLSTEIN, *from whom, after*<-
->*an infinity of persecutions and damages, unjust charges*<-
->*were still being extorted by force in a house that should*<-
->*have been free and privileged.*<-

->MY CHILDREN,<-

1. I saw by your letter that you are still being forced to pay the <term>Schatzings</term> <note>From the German "Schatzung," a type of land tax or extraordinary contribution levied by a sovereign.</note> and <term>Logemens</term> <note>Specifically the "quartering" or housing of soldiers, a common and heavy financial burden during 17th-century conflicts.</note>: a situation I cannot remedy except by abandoning everything, as I am resolved to do. Therefore, take out of the dwelling everything you can, leaving the house in God’s keeping.
2. I know well that our neighbor is not capable of looking after the house properly, but because I fear that everything there will be lost, it matters little that we leave it a bit early, since we will surely have to leave it by force anyway; seeing as God will restore the Duke <note>Likely the Duke of Holstein-Gottorp, who was frequently displaced during the Northern Wars; Bourignon tied her own legal and physical security to his political restoration.</note> to the same extent that He restores me, and will return his property to the same extent that He returns mine.

<summary>In this page, Antoinette Bourignon concludes a letter on the spiritual merit of suffering and begins another advising her followers in Holstein to abandon their property to avoid unjust taxes and the burden of quartering soldiers.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Holstein, taxes, religious persecution, military quartering, Duke of Holstein-Gottorp, spiritual resignation</keywords>

--- Page 277 ---
<meta>This page continues Letter 60, written to Bourignon's associates remaining in the Duchy of Holstein, and begins Letter 61, a personal rebuke to a former member of her household. The text reflects Bourignon’s increasing sense of being a rejected prophet.</meta>

3. And I still see no sign of being restored <note>Bourignon uses the term "restituée" here to refer to the legal restoration of her property and her right to reside in the territory of Holstein-Gottorp.</note>: on the contrary, I am still being slandered while my property is withheld from me. This cannot bring God's blessing upon this Country, to which He sent me with promises of blessing. But one may now make over the country of HOLSTEIN the same lamentations that Jesus Christ made over the City of Jerusalem, saying: *Jerusalem, who kills the Prophets and stones those who are sent to you! Oh, if only you knew the misfortunes that are to come upon you!* <note>Bourignon adapts the words of Jesus from Matthew 23:37 and Luke 19:42, positioning herself as a modern-day prophet whose rejection will trigger divine punishment upon the region.</note> I fear all these same things for Holstein, since I have been driven out of it by force of persecutions and damages. Those who refuse to believe it may taste it to their own detriment. Therefore, try to withdraw from there quietly, without a fuss.....

July 19, 1678.

I should know the reason why. If our kitchen <note>Bourignon refers to the communal dining and household management of her spiritual circle, which followed a strict "rule of necessity" (living only on essentials).</note> did not satisfy you, you could have easily found a better one, since I will not change for anyone the rule of necessity that we observe. It seems to me that the Devil has a hold over you; that, being an enemy of your own good, he puts into your imagination the desire to leave a person who has wished you so much good. By this means, he will easily find a good opportunity to plunge you into all sorts of misfortunes without you even perceiving it.

2. For if you think to speak ill of a person whom you have so praised and esteemed in your public writings, everyone will believe that you have become a fool, and that all the knowledge <note>original: "sciences." In the 17th century, this term often referred to spiritual insights or scholarly learning rather than modern science.</note> that God has given you are but the follies of your imagination. This will cause the works you believed to have come from God himself to be despised. This is why it seems to me that it would be good to put water in your wine <note>A French idiom meaning to temper one's passions or to moderate one's behavior.</note>, and not to let the passions you have against me burst forth. If, in fact, you no longer wish to love or esteem me, you are free to do so: do whatever you please. For my part, I value the contempt of men as much as their praise. After having conquered the world, I no longer have any regard but for pleasing God; and various persons who were good friends to me, having become my enemies, have not caused a single hair to fall from my head, and I have remained after their enmities just as I was before them.

# LETTER LXII.

*To the Lord of the place, who having himself revealed to this Lady out of confidence and friendship the betrayals of her enemy, she gives him advice not to let himself be preoccupied by his slanders and lies.*

->SIR,<-

<margin>1.</margin> I have compassion for the state in which M. D. L. C. <note>Likely Monsieur de Lortie, a former associate of Bourignon’s who had turned against her.</note> now finds himself. It seems to me that through his high speculations <note>In 17th-century mystical writing, "speculations" referred to an overly intellectual or philosophical approach to faith, which Bourignon believed could lead to spiritual pride or madness.</note> the Devil has had the power to trouble his mind: since his actions and words do not even possess natural reasoning. He says he wants to show everyone that I do not have the Spirit of God. In this, he supports my own designs: since I strive here to hide this happiness, keeping myself as hidden and unknown as possible, because the belief that the people of this country might have regarding the graces <note>original: "graces." Spiritual favors or gifts bestowed by God.</note> that God grants me would rob me of my time and rest

In this, one may note his extravagance <note>In this context, "extravagance" implies wandering from the path of reason or balance.</note>. When I left his house in Hamburg, I left him wine, beer, and other provisions for living, and my friends in that area supplied whatever he lacked. I paid for his entire journey coming from Hamburg to Lutzburg <note>Lütetsburg, the location of Baron von Knyphausen’s castle in East Friesland.</note>, where I received him as I would my father, my brother, or my child, having washed his linens myself without the help of anyone. # LETTER LXIII.

*Description of the frenzies of the same persecutor; which would not have been noted were it not that he not only went about slandering this Lady to persons in authority as if she had said or done things to their prejudice; but also that a year later the Authors of her last persecution intended to use the testimony of this frantic man, to whom they wrote (as well as to others) in Hamburg, suggesting to him, when he was no longer thinking of it, to come to East Friesland <note>original: "Oostfrize" — a region in the northwest of Germany where Bourignon sought refuge.</note> to accuse Madlle. Bourignon of witchcraft <note>original: "forcellerie" — likely a variant or misspelling of "sorcellerie" (witchcraft).</note> and other crimes, to produce his absurd pretensions, and to demand a warrant against her person and her goods, to exterminate her and all that belonged to her.*

->MONSIEUR,<-

<margin>1:</margin>
I do not believe that any persons of good sense will give credit to a person who has a troubled mind as Bertrand D. L. C. now has, who is a complete <term>Hypochondriac</term> <note>In the 17th century, "hypochondria" was not just health anxiety but a form of clinical melancholy or delusional disorder thought to originate in the organs of the upper abdomen.</note> if ever there was one in the world. For he says that he is another Moses; and lifting the hair at the front of his head, he says to me, *See the two horns of the two bald patches*, which he has on the sides of his forehead, as many people have like him <note>This refers to the common iconographic depiction of Moses with horns, resulting from a historical mistranslation of the Hebrew word for "radiant" or "rays of light" in the Vulgate Bible.</note>. He carries in the depths of his soul such a great esteem for himself, that he believes himself to be

to say and to believe that he has as much wisdom as

Finally, I know in my conscience that I have never said or done the slightest thing to cause discord between these people. I would have to have a wicked soul indeed to apply myself to creating dissension between a husband and wife. What profit could I gain from such a task? I could not earn money by it, which I also do not need. As for the natural love of men, I have always rejected and despised it: would I, at the age of 64 years, begin to love a married man, or draw him to me and turn him away from his wife? Such a belief should not be held. For even if he were a man as beautiful and wise as an Angel, I would not wish to look at him for such purposes, were he King or Emperor of the whole world; since I have a Spouse <note>The author refers to Christ as her "Spouse," a common term in mystical theology to describe the soul's union with the Divine.</note> who gives me more internal contentment than the carnal and sensual pleasures of the whole world could give me.

4. And therefore Madame ** and others can well remain at rest on that score. If I had wanted to have a man, I would not have had to come looking for one in Luxembourg <note>original: "Lutzbourg"</note>: These opportunities have followed me closely enough against my will. I am as innocent of the suspicions <note>original: "soupçons"</note> held against me as an unborn child. And I would rather death come to me than to cause dissension between anyone at all, if it is not that war which Jesus

Christ said he came to bring, namely, war against evil and sin. For the rest, I love and wish peace to all, just as I wish it for myself. And if La Coste <note>Bertrand de Lortie, sieur de la Coste, was a former disciple of Bourignon who later became one of her most bitter critics, accusing her of heresy and sorcery.</note> had not bewitched the mind of this Lady <note>Referring to the noblewoman mentioned on the previous page whose marriage was reportedly strained.</note> to make her believe a lie, it would be impossible for her to believe what she is told by a man of troubled spirit, over whom the Devil has much influence.

My friends were obliged to watch over him for several nights, as he said that the Devil troubled his mind while he slept with high speculations <note>In this context, "speculations" refers to intense, often obsessive, intellectual or philosophical theorizing.</note> that he did not know how to resolve; and this took away all his rest for the night. This is why he begged to be woken often, as was done. He suffered a severe illness, which I thought came from the common infection of the air <note>A reference to the "miasma theory" of disease, which held that illnesses like the plague or flu were caused by "corrupt" or "infected" air.</note>. On this basis, I took the opportunity to admonish him, as I had done several times before, saying to him, "Sir, be on your guard: the Devil is attempting to trouble your mind to discredit everything you have accomplished throughout your life." He told me that he felt clearly that the Devil had this intention, but that he could not stop himself from studying despite it. I told him: "Sir, if you do not cease these studies and intense speculations, the Devil will disturb your spirit." He replied to me: "I believe it well; he wages war enough against me." And he remained persistent until he said, like Lucifer, that he had reached the wisdom of God, and that God possessed nothing more than he did. From that time on, I could no longer listen to his talk; perceiving this, he conceived a rage against me that makes him vomit all sorts of calumnies <note>False and malicious statements intended to damage someone's reputation; slanders.</note>.

6. I understand that he has also written you a letter to slander me and make me hated. If you find it appropriate for me to publicly refute him, I will do so through the press; or if you prefer to despise him and pass it over in silence, I will do that as well, knowing that men do not have the...

Mary only came to me out of necessity. And for my part, I have never paid a visit to anyone since I have been in Lutzbourg <note>Lutzbourg refers to Lütetsburg in East Friesland, where Bourignon sought refuge.</note>. Thus, I ought not to have offended anyone to be so hated, spied upon, and despised based on the reports of a troubled, hateful, and vindictive man, who day and night thinks of how he might defame me everywhere based on his mere allegations, without being able to prove a single thing he alleges. As for me, I care very little whether people have a good or bad opinion of me, since I have undertaken to wish to please God alone. But I would nevertheless be happy to be able to remain in peace in my lodgings and with my superiors, and that no one should offend God through slander. I fear that the Princesses <note>Likely referring to the local nobility or the Princess of East Friesland, whose protection Bourignon relied upon.</note> will also have heard of L.C. <note>Bertrand de Lortie, known as La Coste, a former associate turned bitter enemy.</note>, since I am told he writes to every side. If I could see what he writes, I could respond to it; but not knowing his allegations, I cannot form an answer to them. I wish someone would make him prove his words, of which I am told he is afraid I might find out. If he spoke the truth, he would fear nothing. As for me, I am quite willing for everyone to see this present letter, since there is nothing I cannot prove by living witnesses worthy of faith; which is assured to you by she who calls herself,

->Sir,<-

->Your most humble Servant,<-

From Lutzbourg, this 3rd of Nov. 1679.

### ANTHOINETTE BOURIGNON.

For eight weeks now, I have had a quartan fever <term>quartan fever: a form of malaria where the fever returns every fourth day; it was often debilitating and long-lasting</term> which keeps me in bed.

# LETTER LXIV.
## *On Afflictions caused by her sick followers.* <note>In Bourignon’s spiritual vocabulary, "sick" often refers to those struggling with their "corrupt nature" or spiritual failings, though she is also describing her own physical illness here.</note>

->SIR,<-

1. I was very pleased to see by your last letter of September 2nd, that you give me the freedom to act as I see fit with the printing press if I see that the two separate houses do not succeed well: for I have already noticed since my illness (which has kept me bedridden for about three months), that men are still too fragile to govern themselves, and that a good sailor is needed to steer the ship of their lives; otherwise, they are in danger of shipwreck by giving free rein to the old habits of their corrupt nature when they have the liberty to do so.

2... My illness was the cause of this delay, having spent eleven weeks confined to bed by a very violent <term>quartan fever</term> <note>original: "fievre quarte" — a form of malaria where paroxysms occur every fourth day. It was notoriously difficult to cure in the 17th century.</note>, which robs me of my judgment every time the fever-heat takes hold of me, and reduces me to the state of a child, without sense. But as soon as the fever leaves me, at that very instant, I return to my right mind as before. If Mr. ** knew of some <term>kitchen medicine</term> <note>original: "medecine de cuisine" — simple, home-prepared remedies or restorative foods rather than professional apothecary drugs.</note> to strengthen me, I would use it gladly. For I cannot stand on my legs because of weakness; I must be dragged wherever they want me to go. It seems that God is making me com—

There, having examined everything, they jumped into my room, drawing the bed curtains, and cried out: *“Well now, Mademoiselle, are you sick? We are from Monsieur de Lutzbourg <note>A local official or nobleman; their claim of being "from" him suggests they were asserting legal or social authority to search the house.</note>, do you not wish to speak to us?”* And as I spoke while trembling greatly, *“I have the fever,”* being thus covered with all the cushions <note>original: "careaux" — large square pillows or cushions used for bedding.</note> in the room, shaking and unable to speak, they rummaged through my entire room; and then they said, *“Come: she is in a severe fever.”* From there they entered Tiellens's <note>Peter Tiellens, a merchant from Hamburg and one of Bourignon's most devoted followers.</note> room, threw his spectacles <note>original: "lunnettes"</note> down in mockery, and leafed through his writings and papers: then they said to Vande Velde: *“Lead us into the other house: we must see that as well:”* to which he excused himself on account of his infirmity, saying that he could not bear the [cold] air. ### MY LORD,

<margin>1.</margin> I cannot describe to you the insults and miseries that I have suffered during your absence. They wanted to come and murder <note>The original French *meurtrir* in this period often specifically meant to kill or assassinate, rather than just to bruise.</note> me in my bed, while I was sick to death; which the murderers themselves have confessed to us, having come several times since during the night, one with an axe, the other with a sword. But God did not permit the thing to take its effect. And now two men are here, fully armed with pistols, an axe, a sword, and carbines, resembling Germans, saying they come from the King of Denmark to search for a man they call the *President of Altona* <note>Altona, a city near Hamburg, was under Danish administration at this time. Bourignon had previously lived there, and her enemies often used her past connections to stir up political trouble.</note>, whom they want to take by force from my lodgings, even though none of us has ever seen or known him, much less has he entered our home. I believe that this is a pretext to come and murder me; for one of them stayed almost an entire day in the house of Master Jacques with the muzzle of his carbine turned toward our garden to see if they might spot a woman there, in order to shoot her, while shouting a thousand insults at me, saying they were waiting for someone else from Bremen <note>A major Hanseatic city in Northern Germany.</note> to come to their aid: that they do not wish to let me live: that I must die, and be burned.

2. I do not know by whom they may have been sent, whether by L. C. <note>As noted previously, this refers to a former associate turned enemy.</note>, by the priests, or other superiors. This is why I have left the house until I hear of your intention. If it is that I withdraw <note>original: "retire"</note>

## SIR,

I cannot describe to you the affronts and miseries I have suffered during your absence. They wanted to come murder <note>original: "meurtrir" — in 17th-century French, this term often meant to kill or slaughter, rather than its modern meaning of "to bruise."</note> me in my bed while I was sick unto death: which the murderers themselves confessed to us, having come several times during the night since then, one with an axe, the other with a sword. And now two men are here, fully armed with pistols, an axe, a sword, and carbines <term>carabines</term> <note>A shorter, lighter firearm used by cavalry and soldiers in the 17th century.</note>, resembling Germans, saying they come from the King of Denmark to search for a man they name *President of Altona* <note>Altona was a city in the Duchy of Holstein, under the administration of the Danish King at the time.</note>, whom they want to seize by force from my lodging, although none of us have ever seen or known him, much less has he entered our home. 2. I do not know by whom they may have been sent, whether by L. ## LETTER LXVII.

*She must hide, and speak to no one, for fear of putting her life in peril. That it is impossible to be saved in the world.*

### SIR,

I have seen by your letter the great desire you have to leave the world to devote yourself to serving God: which is a very good resolution: since I cannot see by what means a single person will be saved while following the ways of the world, where almost all people go by the *broad way* <note>A reference to Matthew 7:13: "Enter through the narrow gate. For wide is the gate and broad is the road that leads to destruction."</note>, which Jesus Christ says

<meta>catchword: leads</meta>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues Letter LXVII from page 254. Antoinette Bourignon is explaining her need for seclusion due to physical threats and the spiritual sufficiency of her written works.</meta>

<header>Of the Just. Letter 68.</header>

*leads to perdition.* <note>Completing the reference to Matthew 7:13: "...and broad is the road that leads to destruction."</note> Regarding the request you make to speak with me, I cannot properly grant it to you, since I must remain hidden and unknown because of my enemies, who seek to take my life: and if they knew for certain where I am, I believe I would not escape. This is why I pray you not to ask me for such a dangerous thing. You may read my writings as you wish, and through them speak to me every day: since what I write, what I speak, and what I practice are the same thing; and there is nothing rare to see in my person. I am a simple creature like any other, who affects no gestures or words of devotion, which often savor of hypocrisy. If God wills that I speak to you, He will certainly allow it in His own time, to whom I recommend you, remaining,

->Sir,<-
->*Your very humble servant.*<-
This 18th of March 1680.
->ANTOINETTE BOURIGNON.<-

usage as Jesus Christ has chosen. 4. I would rejoice greatly to find a person who loved discomfort and suffering to follow Jesus Christ: I would value them more than all the treasures of the world. But this happiness has not yet happened to me, which, in my opinion, is why the Children of God do not know how to find a place of rest to gather, and must still walk like Pilgrims on the earth until God ordains otherwise, and disposes souls TO NO LONGER SEEK REST IN THIS WORLD: THEN THEY WILL FIND IT; NOT BEFORE; since it is necessary that a person suffer in this valley of miseries, where Christ never rested, but suffered even unto the death of the cross, to give us an example. One is quite willing to follow him to Mount Tabor, where all things are pleasant; but not upon Mount Calvary, where there is only sadness and abandonment; <note>In Christian tradition, Mount Tabor is the site of the Transfiguration (divine glory), while Calvary is the site of the Crucifixion (divine suffering). # LETTER LXX.

*Regarding the final persecution at Lutzbourg in East
Frisia, from where, not knowing where to retreat in safety,
sick and exhausted by this last persecution, she fell
into a relapse which was followed by her
death shortly thereafter.*
<note>Lutzbourg refers to Lütetsburg Castle in East Frisia, where Antoinette Bourignon had sought refuge under the protection of Baron Dodo von Knyphausen. This letter, written in 1680, captures her final days of exhaustion and illness.</note>

->SIR,<-

<margin>1.</margin> I have finally been forced to flee the persecution of my enemies. This is something I had foreseen for some time. It is a pity to see how great the powers of darkness are. Those who had taken me under their protection with such affection are now seeking to have me apprehended by the law. What unfaithfulness! from a man who, by a vow and with tears in his eyes, had dedicated himself entirely to God, offering to employ all the goods he had, or would have in the future, for His glory! While he now works to take away from the Children of God what they have dedicated to His service. It is a derangement of mind brought about by his wife, and God would be right to say to him as He did to Adam: *Because thou hast hearkened unto the voice of thy wife, and hast eaten of the fruit of which I commanded thee, saying, Thou shalt not eat of it: cursed is the ground for thy sake* <note>original: "Parce que tu as écouté la voix de ta femme..." quoting Genesis 3:17.</note>: For he can expect nothing but every curse, by persecuting a Servant of God, of whom it is written, *He that toucheth you toucheth the apple of my eye.* <note>original: "Qui vous touche, il touche la prunelle de mes yeux," quoting Zechariah 2:8.</note>

<margin>2.</margin> I thought of coming all the way to you; but in con-

<sig>R 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: fide-</meta>

<summary>Antoinette Bourignon describes her forced flight from Lütetsburg due to legal threats from her former protector, whom she believes has been misled by his wife. She frames her personal betrayal as a spiritual battle, comparing her persecutor to Adam and herself to a protected servant of God.</summary>
<keywords>persecution, Lütetsburg, East Frisia, betrayal, powers of darkness, Adam, Servant of God, Baron von Knyphausen</keywords>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page continues Letter LXX (70). Bourignon is describing her flight from East Frisia following the betrayal by Baron Dodo von Knyphausen and his wife. She is currently in a state of uncertainty, hiding from those who seek to arrest or defame her.</meta>

<page-num>260</page-num>
<header>The Persecutions</header>

Considering that I also have various enemies in those parts, I have stopped on my journey, where I heard that ** <note>The double asterisks likely refer to the Baroness von Knyphausen, whom Bourignon blamed for the Baron's change of heart.</note> has sent an express messenger to Frisia to the homes of people known to me, to find out if they have anything to charge me with, or wish to say anything ill of me; and specifically if they have heard me say anything bad about **, apparently in order to join all these evils against me along with those of which ** wishes to accuse me—who, in the manner of Potiphar’s wife, accuses another of the crime of which she herself is guilty, just as that immodest woman accused the chaste Joseph of her own lewdness. <note>A reference to Genesis 39, where Potiphar’s wife unsuccessfully attempted to seduce Joseph and then falsely accused him of attempted rape when he fled.</note> Perhaps God will, in His own time, make the truth known, defending my innocence. I would very much like you to give me your advice to know where I might best stay. I had ordered the renting of some secluded house in Amsterdam, from which I would never go out, so as to remain unknown. I am still holding this plan in suspense until you have advised me on the matter. I could well remain in Frisia, or go somewhere in some town or village in Holland; but I do not know if I could remain as hidden and unknown there as in the great city, where people take less notice of strangers. I could better take care of my <term>works</term> <note>referring to her various manuscripts and published books</note>; but I must not risk my person for that. My brothers <note>Her spiritual followers or associates.</note> have written to me that you are continuing your pious designs to have the **Gospel of the Kingdom** preached: for which I thank God, and wish to find people fit to go and publish it throughout the whole world.

<meta>catchword: 3. God</meta>

<summary>Antoinette Bourignon describes being pursued by false accusations from her former protectors and weighs the benefits of hiding in a large city like Amsterdam versus the countryside. She remains committed to publishing her works and spreading her "Gospel of the Kingdom" despite her physical danger.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Letter LXX (70). Having fled the betrayal of the Baron von Knyphausen in East Frisia, Antoinette Bourignon is writing from a place of temporary safety in Franeker, finalizing her thoughts on the spiritual nature of her persecutions and her plans for correspondence.</meta>

<page-num>261</page-num>
<header>Of the Just. Letter 70.</header>

3. God always abounds more and more in His lights. <note>In Bourignon’s vocabulary, "lights" refer to divine inspirations or spiritual understandings.</note> This is the reason why the Devil so strongly induces the wicked to persecute me, or at least to disturb my peace. This, however, they are unable to do, since God, displaying His Omnipotence, breaks all the instruments they set against me, as the Prophet predicted. I always discover in time the snares that Satan has set against me; and I avoid them gently with peace. You may write to me here safely. I am staying with a faithful friend who does not understand French; it is only a pity that this man has married a woman who prevents him from flying to God. In this, you have a great advantage; since all women are "Eves" who withdraw their husbands from communion with God, just as our old mother Eve did. Congratulating you on being delivered from this breed, <note>Bourignon, despite being a woman, often wrote disparagingly of her own sex and viewed marriage as a trap that hindered the soul's ascent to God.</note> I remain forever,

->Sir,<-

->*Your very humble servant, whom you know.*<-

This September 27 / October 7, 1680. <note>The double date reflects the discrepancy between the Julian and Gregorian calendars, which were both in use in different regions of Europe.</note>
