15. *The Holy Visor.* 10 sheets. <note>original: "La Sainte Visiere." A "visor" or "eyeshade" here likely refers to a spiritual guide or a way of focusing one's vision on divine truth.</note>

in three parts: 47 sheets.

The following books will, if it please God, be published next year, 1684. <note>Bourignon died in 1680; these were works being prepared for publication from her remaining manuscripts by her followers.</note>

19. *The Testimony of Truth.* Second part. 35 sheets.

23. *The Call of God and the Refusal of Men:* Second part. 17 sheets.
24. *The Confusion of the Builders of Babel.*
25. *Wholesome Counsels:* in 2 volumes.

These are all the Works of the late Miss BOURIGNON: and there are no more others to be expected.

Most of her Works are translated from the French Original into German and Flemish, and all will henceforth be available in these two languages. *Solid Virtue*, first part, *The Touchstone*, and *The Renewal of the Gospel Spirit*, first part, are also available in Latin. <note>original: "Latin." The translation of her works into Latin, German, and Flemish (Dutch) demonstrates her wide international influence in the 17th-century Republic of Letters.</note>

All these books can be found in AMSTERDAM, at the shop of PIERRE ARENTZ, on Exchange Street, and also at JEAN RIEWERTZ, in the same street. And they will be found at the fairs in FRANKFURT at the shop of HERMAN VON SAND, Bookseller. <note>The Frankfurt Book Fair was already a central hub for European publishing in the 1600s.</note>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page concludes the document with Antoinette Bourignon’s formal "Confession of Faith." Written in 1675 while she was in Schleswig, it serves as a legal and spiritual defense against accusations of heresy or heterodoxy. It follows the catalog of her works to prove to her readers and the authorities that her writings remained within the bounds of traditional Christian doctrine.</meta>

## Confession of Faith of Miss Bourignon, which she presented to the Court of Gottorf, to oppose the slanderous rumors that the Clergy were spreading to cast doubt on the purity of her Faith. <note>The Court of Gottorf was the residence of the Dukes of Holstein-Gottorp; Bourignon sought refuge in their territories during her travels through Northern Europe.</note>

<margin>1</margin>
I am a Christian, and I believe everything that a true Christian must believe.

<margin>2</margin>
I am baptized in the Catholic Church in the Name of the Father, in the Name of the Son, and in the Name of the Holy Spirit.

<margin>3</margin>
I believe the twelve Articles of the *creed* <note>original: "credo"</note>, or the Apostles' Creed, and do not doubt any article thereof.

<margin>4</margin>
I believe that **Jesus Christ** is True God, and that he is also True Man, and that he is the Savior and Redeemer of the World.

<margin>5</margin>
I believe in the Gospel, in the Holy Prophets, and in all Holy Scripture, both the Old and the New Testament.
And I wish to live and die in all points of this belief: which I declare before God and men to all those whom it may concern.
In witness whereof I have signed this my True Confession with my hand, and sealed it with my seal. At *Schleswig* <note>original: "Sleeswick," the capital of the Duchy of Schleswig.</note>, March 11, 1675.

->**ANTHOINETTE BOURIGNON.**<-

<term>Place of the Seal</term> <note>original: "L: S." (Locus Sigilli), a standard marking on printed legal documents indicating where the author’s wax seal was placed on the original manuscript.</note>

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>This page presents Antoinette Bourignon’s 1675 formal declaration of faith, intended to clear her name of heresy charges by affirming her belief in the Trinity, the Apostles' Creed, and the divinity of Christ. It serves as a defensive legal postscript to the collection of her works.</summary>
<keywords>Antoinette Bourignon, Confession of Faith, Gottorf, Schleswig, Apostles' Creed, Trinity, religious defense, 17th-century Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page is the title page of a spiritual guidebook titled "The Touchstone." It follows Antoinette Bourignon’s personal "Confession of Faith" from the previous page, transitioning from a defense of her own beliefs to a practical manual intended to guide her followers in the "Order of the Christian Life." This work was published in Amsterdam in 1679, during a period when the city was a center for religious non-conformist printing.</meta>

# THE TOUCHSTONE, <note>original: "Pierre de Touche"; a touchstone is a piece of Jasper or similar dark stone used to test the purity of gold and silver. Here, it is used metaphorically as a standard to test the authenticity of one's spiritual life.</note>

->To recognize<-

# the Order of the Christian Life,

Wherein one treats the examination of conscience, a
general confession, the manner of receiving Com-
munion well, the Love of God,
trust in God, the manner of preparing for
death, & an easy method for rising
to a high perfection. With many other prayers,
new and <unclear>useful</unclear>.

---

<image-desc>A printer's device featuring a large lily <note>original: "fleur-de-lis," the heraldic lily associated with French royalty but also a common printer's mark</note> within a shield-shaped frame. The shield is surrounded by faint text; fragments on the left side read "OF THE LILY" <note>original: "DE LA LIS"</note> and text at the bottom appears to read "ON THE RIVER" <note>original: "SUR LA RIVIERE"</note>.</image-desc>

->Amsterdam, [published] by Pierre Arents, bookseller on<-
->the Dam, in the Street of the Exchange. 1679.<- <note>The "Dam" is Amsterdam's central square, and the "Rue de la Bourse" (Beursstraat) was the street of the Stock Exchange; this area was the heart of the city's commercial and publishing district.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for "The Touchstone," a 1679 devotional manual providing instructions on confession, communion, and attaining spiritual perfection. It was published in Amsterdam by Pierre Arents and reflects the practical, instructional side of Antoinette Bourignon's religious movement.</summary>
<keywords>The Touchstone, Christian Life, Pierre Arents, Amsterdam, devotional manual, spiritual perfection, examination of conscience, 17th-century printing</keywords>

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>Bleed-through from the reverse side of the page is visible throughout the paper. This is the title page of a work by Antoinette Bourignon (1616–1680), a prominent and controversial Flemish mystic who claimed direct inspiration from God and founded several religious communities. Her works were often published in Amsterdam, a center for religious tolerance and printing in the 17th century.</meta>

->The<-

# TOUCHSTONE,

<note>A touchstone is a dark stone, such as basanite, used to test the purity of gold and silver by the color of the streak they leave when rubbed on it. Here, it is used metaphorically as a standard for spiritual truth.</note>

->To distinguish<-

# the Gold of true Charity,

### from the Metal of gilded Copper of apparent Charity.

<note>original: "Charité." In 17th-century religious texts, "charity" refers not just to giving alms, but to "Caritas"—the supreme theological virtue of selfless, divine love for God and neighbor.</note>

And to explain what the Merits of our Savior Jesus Christ consist of; and the true Love of God. Also declaring the Glorious state in which all men were created in Adam. And how they must live to return to that same perfection. With many marvelous divine Mysteries.

->By<-

## ANTOINETTE BOURIGNON.

*To contradict the lies and calumnies spread about her and her writings by a certain Mr. GEORGE HENRY BURCHARDUS, Minister of the Lutheran reformation, in the Cathedral Church of Schleswig.* <note>Schleswig is a city in northern Germany. Bourignon frequently clashed with established church authorities, both Catholic and Protestant.</note> *In order to open the eyes of all men of good will in these dangerous times; when evil is commonly taken for good, and lies for the truth.*

---

### THE COAT OF ARMS

<image-desc>A heraldic shield containing a fleur-de-lis. Around the inner border of the shield, text is printed: "OF THE LILY IN FLANDERS" vertically on the left side, "OF THE CITY OF LILLE" vertically on the right side, and "ON THE RIVER" at the bottom, printed upside down.</image-desc>

->In Amsterdam, at Pierre Arentz, Bookseller at the<-
->Three Ships, on Exchange Street. 1679.<-

<summary>This title page introduces a polemical work by the mystic Antoinette Bourignon, designed to defend her teachings against Lutheran criticism and define the nature of true divine love. The page establishes the work's spiritual authority through the metaphor of a "touchstone" and provides publication details from the Dutch Golden Age.</summary>
